### Efficient Compositional Multi-tasking for On-device Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16083v1)
- **Authors**: Ondrej Bohdal, Mete Ozay, Jijoong Moon, Kyeng-Hun Lee, Hyeonmok Ko, Umberto Michieli
- **Abstract**: Adapter parameters provide a mechanism to modify the behavior of machine learning models and have gained significant popularity in the context of large language models (LLMs) and generative AI. These parameters can be merged to support multiple tasks via a process known as task merging. However, prior work on merging in LLMs, particularly in natural language processing, has been limited to scenarios where each test example addresses only a single task. In this paper, we focus on on-device settings and study the problem of text-based compositional multi-tasking, where each test example involves the simultaneous execution of multiple tasks. For instance, generating a translated summary of a long text requires solving both translation and summarization tasks concurrently. To facilitate research in this setting, we propose a benchmark comprising four practically relevant compositional tasks. We also present an efficient method (Learnable Calibration) tailored for on-device applications, where computational resources are limited, emphasizing the need for solutions that are both resource-efficient and high-performing. Our contributions lay the groundwork for advancing the capabilities of LLMs in real-world multi-tasking scenarios, expanding their applicability to complex, resource-constrained use cases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of compositional multi-tasking for on-device large language models (LLMs).  Compositional multi-tasking involves simultaneously performing multiple tasks, like generating a translated summary, within a single inference pass. This is crucial for resource-constrained environments like mobile devices.  The authors introduce a new benchmark comprising four practically relevant compositional tasks. They also propose a novel and efficient method called Learnable Calibration, which combines pre-existing task-specific adapters with a small number of learnable parameters.  This approach aims to achieve high performance with minimal computational and storage overhead.  The paper demonstrates that existing methods, including standard merging techniques, are inadequate for this compositional setting and that Learnable Calibration provides a significant improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in defining and tackling the specific challenge of *compositional* multi-tasking within the constraints of on-device LLMs.  While adapter merging and PEFT techniques are established, the focus on *simultaneous* multi-tasking, as opposed to switching between tasks, is a valuable contribution. The benchmark creation is also a novel component, providing a standardized way to evaluate such methods.  The Learnable Calibration method itself is a relatively incremental improvement upon adapter merging techniques, but it's well-motivated by the limitations identified.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the paper stems from its practical relevance.  Enabling complex multi-tasking capabilities on resource-constrained devices significantly expands the applicability of LLMs in real-world scenarios. The benchmark they release will also make it easier for others in the field to research and make gains. The findings are important because the current move towards resource efficient LLMs is a key area to pursue.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the compositional multi-tasking challenge and its importance.
    *   Practical focus: The emphasis on on-device deployment and resource efficiency is commendable.
    *   Benchmark development: The creation of a dedicated benchmark with diverse compositional tasks is a valuable resource for the community.
    *   Solid empirical evaluation:  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results, comparing Learnable Calibration against a range of baselines.
    *   Qualitative analysis: The authors include illustrative examples to highlight the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental method: While effective, Learnable Calibration builds upon existing adapter merging techniques. The core idea is a weighted merging, and calibration of the weighted adapters. It is not a totally novel conceptual leap.
    *   Data dependence: The pre-training of the learnable parameters relies on access to compositional task data, which may not always be available. This is not a serious impediment though, since the authors pre-train the models on a server and then deploy them on-device.
    *   Limited Scale: Though they do evaluate models across varying sizes and complexity, there is not evidence of performance at extremely high parameter counts. This is not a big problem in an on-device context, but it limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   Limited scope in compositional configurations. The authors only focus on chains of up to three LLMs, which is not a very large or complicated configuration.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and capable LLMs for on-device applications.  The benchmark can foster further research in this area. The approach to compositionally multi-tasking has implications for how we view adapter tuning and resource efficient parameter tuning techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a clearly defined problem with a practical solution and a valuable benchmark. The method, while incremental, achieves significant improvements over existing approaches and is well-suited for the target application. The evaluation is thorough, and the results are convincing. The primary limitation is the reliance on a data-dependent stage, but this is a minor issue.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Personalized Image Generation through Social Context Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16095v1)
- **Authors**: Parul Gupta, Abhinav Dhall, Thanh-Toan Do
- **Abstract**: Personalized image generation, where reference images of one or more subjects are used to generate their image according to a scene description, has gathered significant interest in the community. However, such generated images suffer from three major limitations -- complex activities, such as $<$man, pushing, motorcycle$>$ are not generated properly with incorrect human poses, reference human identities are not preserved, and generated human gaze patterns are unnatural/inconsistent with the scene description. In this work, we propose to overcome these shortcomings through feedback-based fine-tuning of existing personalized generation methods, wherein, state-of-art detectors of pose, human-object-interaction, human facial recognition and human gaze-point estimation are used to refine the diffusion model. We also propose timestep-based inculcation of different feedback modules, depending upon whether the signal is low-level (such as human pose), or high-level (such as gaze point). The images generated in this manner show an improvement in the generated interactions, facial identities and image quality over three benchmark datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Improving Personalized Image Generation through Social Context Feedback":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in personalized image generation where reference images are used to generate images according to text descriptions. The authors identify three key shortcomings: poor generation of complex activities, incorrect human poses, inconsistent preservation of human identities, and unnatural gaze patterns. To overcome these issues, they propose a feedback-based fine-tuning approach. This method uses state-of-the-art detectors for pose, human-object interaction, facial recognition, and gaze estimation to refine the diffusion model during training.  The paper also introduces a timestep-based approach for applying different feedback modules based on signal level (low-level vs. high-level cues). They demonstrate improved image quality, interactions, and identity preservation across benchmark datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using feedback from pre-trained detectors for fine-tuning personalized image generation models exhibits some novelty. While using pre-trained detectors is not new in itself, intelligently incorporating their feedback *during* the training process, specifically within a diffusion model's framework for *personalized* generation, is a valuable contribution.  The timestep-aware application of feedback based on the granularity of the signal (e.g., pose vs. gaze) is also a thoughtful addition. The paper’s novelty lies in the holistic approach to addressing multiple shortcomings in social context image generation using tailored feedback mechanisms.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles a significant problem. Generating realistic images of humans interacting with objects and each other in a way that preserves identity and aligns with text prompts is crucial for many applications. If successful, this approach could lead to more controllable and believable generated content. Addressing the limitations in complex scene generation (pose, interaction) is relevant and impactful. The empirical evaluation and the demonstrated improvements across multiple benchmark datasets (HICO-DET, GazeFollow, Concept101) further bolster the significance of the work.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the existing limitations of personalized image generation.
    * **Holistic Approach:** It addresses multiple, interconnected problems (pose, interaction, identity, gaze).
    * **Intelligent Feedback Incorporation:** The timestep-aware feedback system is well-motivated and appears effective.
    * **Solid Empirical Evaluation:** Improvements are demonstrated across multiple datasets with relevant metrics.
    * **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is generally easy to follow, with a clear description of the methodology.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements are noticeable, they might be considered incremental relative to SSR-Encoder, rather than a paradigm shift.  The performance on Concept101, while better, is still quite close to other methods. The gains in some metrics appear to be relatively modest, even when using all the feedback mechanisms.
    * **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:**  The approach relies heavily on the performance of existing detectors (pose, HOI, etc.).  If these detectors have biases or limitations, they will be propagated into the generated images. The robustness to detector errors needs to be more thoroughly examined.
    * **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly stated, fine-tuning a diffusion model with multiple feedback loops can be computationally expensive, and may limit its wider adoption. This should be discussed more thoroughly.
    * **Qualitative Examples:** The qualitative results in Figure 4 are helpful, but could be more compelling. A few more examples showcasing the benefits of each feedback module independently would strengthen the argument.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of personalized image generation research.  The feedback-based fine-tuning approach could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The focus on social context is also likely to gain more attention as the field matures. The timestep-based framework can serve as a template for more refined, knowledge-driven training of diffusion models.
**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and relatively novel approach to improving personalized image generation within the social context. While the improvements are incremental and the approach relies on pre-trained detectors, the work addresses a significant problem and is supported by solid empirical results and a well-reasoned methodology. The timestep-based feedback approach is a notable contribution. The paper is likely to be of interest to the computer vision and graphics communities and has the potential to stimulate further research. A higher score would require more substantial breakthroughs and a more comprehensive analysis of robustness and computational cost.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Expert-Guided LLM Reasoning for Battery Discovery: From AI-Driven Hypothesis to Synthesis and Characterization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16110v1)
- **Authors**: Shengchao Liu, Hannan Xu, Yan Ai, Huanxin Li, Yoshua Bengio, Harry Guo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) leverage chain-of-thought (CoT) techniques to tackle complex problems, representing a transformative breakthrough in artificial intelligence (AI). However, their reasoning capabilities have primarily been demonstrated in solving math and coding problems, leaving their potential for domain-specific applications-such as battery discovery-largely unexplored. Inspired by the idea that reasoning mirrors a form of guided search, we introduce ChatBattery, a novel agentic framework that integrates domain knowledge to steer LLMs toward more effective reasoning in materials design. Using ChatBattery, we successfully identify, synthesize, and characterize three novel lithium-ion battery cathode materials, which achieve practical capacity improvements of 28.8%, 25.2%, and 18.5%, respectively, over the widely used cathode material, LiNi0.8Mn0.1Co0.1O2 (NMC811). Beyond this discovery, ChatBattery paves a new path by showing a successful LLM-driven and reasoning-based platform for battery materials invention. This complete AI-driven cycle-from design to synthesis to characterization-demonstrates the transformative potential of AI-driven reasoning in revolutionizing materials discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChatBattery, a novel agentic framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) and domain expertise for the discovery of new battery cathode materials. The framework integrates chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning within specialized agents that guide the LLM through sequential stages of exploration (hypothesis generation and testing) and exploitation (deduplication, ranking, and validation). The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ChatBattery by identifying, synthesizing, and characterizing three new lithium-ion battery cathode materials (NMC-SiMg, NMC-SiCa, and NMC-MgB) that exhibit significantly improved practical capacity compared to the widely used NMC811 cathode. The paper highlights the complete AI-driven cycle, from materials design to synthesis and characterization, showcasing the transformative potential of LLM-powered reasoning for accelerating materials discovery.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of LLMs with expert knowledge to create a reasoning-based framework for battery materials discovery. While LLMs have been applied to scientific domains, the agentic approach, coupled with explicit domain-specific guidance to ensure scientifically valid reasoning, represents a valuable contribution. The successful identification and synthesis of novel cathode materials, despite the complexities of the field, further bolsters the novelty of the approach. However, the use of GPT-3.5, while a capable LLM, is not cutting-edge in 2025. The core idea, rather than a specific technological advancement, is what defines the innovation.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to accelerate the discovery of new materials, particularly those crucial for addressing climate change and energy storage challenges. The demonstrated capacity improvements over NMC811 suggest that this approach can lead to tangible advancements in battery technology. Furthermore, the paper emphasizes the interpretability of the LLM's reasoning process, which fosters trust and facilitates collaboration between AI and human scientists. By creating the full design-to-synthesis-to-characterization cycle, the authors show a roadmap for future exploration. While the performance improvements are appreciable, they are not necessarily revolutionary. The main significance lies in the demonstration of the framework itself.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined framework:** ChatBattery provides a clear and structured methodology for LLM-driven materials discovery.
    *   **Expert guidance:** Integration of domain knowledge ensures scientific validity and relevance of generated hypotheses.
    *   **Experimental validation:** Successful synthesis and characterization of novel cathode materials.
    *   **Performance improvements:** Demonstrated practical capacity improvements over NMC811.
    *   **Interpretability:** Emphasis on transparent and rational explanations for generated choices.
    *   **Full cycle demonstration:** Covering the complete design-to-synthesis-to-characterization cycle.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-3.5:** The reliance on a not leading-edge LLM at the time of writing is a limitation. However, it emphasizes the framework's ability to leverage more generally accessible tools.
    *   **Optimization within known paradigms:** The majority of predicted materials are structurally and compositionally similar to known classes, suggesting a limitation in generating fundamentally new chemistries.
    *   **Room for greater novelty in performance enhancement:** While capacity improves compared to NMC811, more dramatic increases would have further amplified the results.
    *   **Lack of long-term cycle life testing:** While initial electrochemical characterization shows promising results, long-term cycle life testing remains crucial for assessing the practical viability of the materials.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging the development of similar LLM-based frameworks for materials discovery in other domains.
    *   Highlighting the importance of integrating domain expertise into AI-driven scientific processes.
    *   Inspiring further research into LLM interpretability and explainability for scientific applications.
    *   Facilitating faster discovery and optimization processes within battery technology and other related fields.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While the paper presents a valuable and innovative approach to battery materials discovery by integrating LLMs with expert knowledge, the limitations discussed above prevent a higher score. The successful experimental validation and performance improvements are significant strengths. The core novelty lies in the *methodology* of expert-guided LLM reasoning, demonstrating a clear path for future materials design. However, the evolutionary nature of the materials discovered and the lack of long-term cycle life data, keep the score lower. Improvements in the sophistication of the LLM, and exploration of a more expanded chemical design space, would substantially increase the impact and justify a higher rating. The demonstratable, end-to-end AI-driven process is very valuable, but future studies will need to build upon this existing work to truly realize the groundbreaking potential suggested.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### PUSA V1.0: Surpassing Wan-I2V with $500 Training Cost by Vectorized Timestep Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16116v1)
- **Authors**: Yaofang Liu, Yumeng Ren, Aitor Artola, Yuxuan Hu, Xiaodong Cun, Xiaotong Zhao, Alan Zhao, Raymond H. Chan, Suiyun Zhang, Rui Liu, Dandan Tu, Jean-Michel Morel
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of video diffusion models has been hindered by fundamental limitations in temporal modeling, particularly the rigid synchronization of frame evolution imposed by conventional scalar timestep variables. While task-specific adaptations and autoregressive models have sought to address these challenges, they remain constrained by computational inefficiency, catastrophic forgetting, or narrow applicability. In this work, we present Pusa, a groundbreaking paradigm that leverages vectorized timestep adaptation (VTA) to enable fine-grained temporal control within a unified video diffusion framework. Besides, VTA is a non-destructive adaptation, which means it fully preserves the capabilities of the base model. By finetuning the SOTA Wan2.1-T2V-14B model with VTA, we achieve unprecedented efficiency -- surpassing the performance of Wan-I2V-14B with $\leq$ 1/200 of the training cost (\$500 vs. $\geq$ \$100,000) and $\leq$ 1/2500 of the dataset size (4K vs. $\geq$ 10M samples). Pusa not only sets a new standard for image-to-video (I2V) generation, achieving a VBench-I2V total score of 87.32\% (vs. 86.86\% of Wan-I2V-14B), but also unlocks many zero-shot multi-task capabilities such as start-end frames and video extension -- all without task-specific training. Meanwhile, Pusa can still perform text-to-video generation. Mechanistic analyses reveal that our approach preserves the foundation model's generative priors while surgically injecting temporal dynamics, avoiding the combinatorial explosion inherent to vectorized timesteps. This work establishes a scalable, efficient, and versatile paradigm for next-generation video synthesis, democratizing high-fidelity video generation for research and industry alike. Code is open-sourced at https://github.com/Yaofang-Liu/Pusa-VidGen
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "PUSA V1.0: Surpassing WAN-I2V with \$500 Training Cost by Vectorized Timestep Adaptation" introduces a new paradigm for video diffusion models (VDMs) called vectorized timestep adaptation (VTA).  The core idea is to use FVDM's (Frame-Aware Video Diffusion Model) vectorized timesteps within the architecture of a large, pre-trained text-to-video (T2V) model (specifically, Wan2.1-T2V-14B). By fine-tuning this architecture with VTA, the resulting model, Pusa, achieves state-of-the-art image-to-video (I2V) generation performance with significantly reduced training cost ( \$500 vs. \$100,000 for Wan-I2V) and dataset size.  The key benefit of VTA is its non-destructive nature, meaning it retains the original T2V capabilities of the pre-trained model while enabling new features like start-end frame conditioning and video extension *without task-specific training*.  The authors demonstrate through ablation studies and analysis that VTA enables a targeted adaptation, preserving the foundation model's knowledge while surgically injecting temporal dynamics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the successful integration of the vectorized timestep approach (previously explored in FVDM, but not at this scale) into a large, open-source T2V model, and achieving a dramatic improvement in training efficiency. The concept of vectorized timesteps itself isn't new (the paper extensively credits FVDM), but the *application* to an existing SOTA T2V model and the resulting efficiency gains represent a significant practical advancement. The non-destructive adaptation aspect is interesting, as it allows the model to retain its previous capabilities while adding new ones. However, this claim could be strengthened with more specific experiments isolating the impact of the non-destructive nature and ensuring that the previous T2V performance is not degraded by using VTA.

* **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:
    * **Efficiency:** The drastically reduced training cost makes high-fidelity video generation more accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited resources. This democratizes the field and facilitates further innovation.
    * **Multi-Task Capability:** The ability to handle T2V, I2V, and other temporal tasks (start-end frame control, video extension) within a single model is valuable and simplifies the deployment process.  This is a clear advantage over task-specific fine-tuning approaches.
    * **Bridging Theory and Practice:** The paper successfully applies a relatively theoretical idea (vectorized timesteps) to a large, practical model, demonstrating its real-world applicability.
    * **Data Efficiency:** Achieving similar or better results with much less data is beneficial and reduces the burden of curating large datasets.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper clearly describes the method and its advantages.
    * The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the superior performance and efficiency of Pusa.
    * The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of various hyperparameters and design choices.
    * The analysis of attention maps and parameter changes sheds light on the underlying mechanisms of VTA.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The paper mentions that VTA preserves T2V functionality of original model, the actual T2V performance isn't quantitively compared against original WAN-T2V, leading to ambiguity.
    * While the method is simple in concept, the details of the architectural modifications (per-frame modulation) could be more thoroughly explained.
    * The dependence on a pre-trained foundation model means the approach is not entirely "from scratch." This doesn't invalidate the results, but it's worth noting.
    * While the paper highlights the zero-shot generalization capabilities for start-end frame completion and video extension, there aren't thorough quantitative metrics to back up the visual results provided, especially compared to the extensive VBench-I2V results.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the future direction of video diffusion models by emphasizing the importance of efficient and versatile approaches. It could encourage researchers to explore new ways to adapt existing models rather than training them from scratch, and to focus on developing methods that can handle a wide range of video generation tasks.  The reduced training costs could accelerate progress in the field by lowering the barrier to entry.

* **Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:**
The paper presents a significant engineering advancement in the field of video diffusion. It convincingly demonstrates a method (VTA) that achieves state-of-the-art I2V performance with a radical reduction in training costs and data requirements. The paper's clear description, compelling results, and mechanistic analysis make a strong case for its effectiveness. While the core concept of vectorized timesteps isn't entirely new, its successful adaptation to a large pre-trained model and the resulting efficiency gains are noteworthy. The main weakness is the limited quantitative analysis of the non-I2V tasks and a lack of detail on the architectural modifications and better original t2v performance claim support. Addressing those weaknesses, further solidifying the importance and influence of work, could have push its potential to become a true breakthrough.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking LLM Privacy Recognition for Social Robot Decision Making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16124v1)
- **Authors**: Dakota Sullivan, Shirley Zhang, Jennica Li, Heather Kirkorian, Bilge Mutlu, Kassem Fawaz
- **Abstract**: Social robots are embodied agents that interact with people while following human communication norms. These robots interact using verbal and non-verbal cues, and share the physical environments of people. While social robots have previously utilized rule-based systems or probabilistic models for user interaction, the rapid evolution of large language models (LLMs) presents new opportunities to develop LLM-empowered social robots for enhanced human-robot interaction. To fully realize these capabilities, however, robots need to collect data such as audio, fine-grained images, video, and locations. As a result, LLMs often process sensitive personal information, particularly within home environments. Given the tension between utility and privacy risks, evaluating how current LLMs manage sensitive data is critical. Specifically, we aim to explore the extent to which out-of-the-box LLMs are privacy-aware in the context of household social robots. In this study, we present a set of privacy-relevant scenarios crafted through the lens of Contextual Integrity (CI). We first survey users' privacy preferences regarding in-home social robot behaviors and then examine how their privacy orientation affects their choices of these behaviors (N = 450). We then provide the same set of scenarios and questions to state-of-the-art LLMs (N = 10) and find that the agreement between humans and LLMs is low. To further investigate the capabilities of LLMs as a potential privacy controller, we implement four additional prompting strategies and compare their results. Finally, we discuss the implications and potential of AI privacy awareness in human-robot interaction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the privacy awareness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of social robots operating within home environments. The authors use a Contextual Integrity (CI) framework to create privacy-relevant scenarios and then benchmark several state-of-the-art LLMs against human privacy preferences collected via surveys. They find a low agreement between human expectations and LLM responses, and investigate the impact of different prompting strategies to improve LLM conformity. Ultimately, the study suggests that while LLMs have a general sense of privacy, they lack nuanced privacy awareness needed for social robots. The authors propose design implications for LLM-powered robots, emphasizing naturalistic user interactions to learn privacy preferences over time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant and Timely Topic:** The intersection of LLMs, robotics, and privacy is a crucial and rapidly evolving area. As LLMs become more prevalent in robotics, understanding their ability to handle sensitive data is essential.
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The authors conduct a series of user studies to identify human privacy preferences, then benchmark multiple LLMs using various prompting strategies. This multi-faceted approach strengthens the validity of their findings.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The use of the Contextual Integrity framework provides a solid theoretical foundation for developing privacy scenarios. The study design incorporates both quantitative (surveys, F1 scores) and qualitative (case studies of LLM reasoning) analyses. The user studies are well-powered, with sufficient sample sizes.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers concrete design implications for developing LLM-powered robots that prioritize user privacy, particularly around continuous learning of user preferences.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability of Scenarios:** While the scenarios are grounded in the Contextual Integrity framework, their design could be affected by the authors' biases on what is considered 'private'. The LLM's understanding of 'private' can be affected by cultural and individual differences, which are not fully accounted for.
    *   **Limited LLM Reasoning Analysis:** The case study section provides useful insights into the reasoning of two LLMs, but the scope is limited. A deeper, more systematic analysis of LLM rationales across all models and scenarios could reveal more patterns and underlying mechanisms.
    *   **Lack of novel approach for evaluating LLMs:** The study evaluates LLM via standard and traditional approach, hence lacking novel evaluation methods or metrics for the study.
    *   **POS Scale Discrepancy:** The difference in POS scores, compared to previous research, raises questions about the representativeness of the participant sample or potential biases introduced by the study's framing. The clustering identified in the study also lacks the three previously identified clusters.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The study is one of the first to systematically evaluate the privacy awareness of LLMs specifically in the context of human-robot interaction within home environments.
    *   The framework of combining Contextual Integrity with user studies and LLM benchmarking provides a valuable template for future research in this area.
    *   The identification of strengths and weaknesses of different prompting strategies offers practical guidance for developers of LLM-powered robots.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It will encourage more rigorous evaluations of LLM privacy awareness in various application domains.
    *   It will stimulate the development of improved prompting techniques and training methods to enhance LLM privacy sensitivity.
    *   It will inform the design of privacy-preserving architectures for social robots that leverage LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the emerging field of LLM-powered robotics and their privacy implications. It offers a well-defined methodology, empirical evidence, and practical design recommendations. While it does have limitations in terms of potential biases in scenario design, scope of reasoning analysis, and explanation of inconsistent POS scale findings, it is a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research and development efforts. The prompt engineering also offers an insights. The originality in terms of the framework for the study is high, though the results were more incremental than transformational.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Disability Across Cultures: A Human-Centered Audit of Ableism in Western and Indic LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16130v1)
- **Authors**: Mahika Phutane, Aditya Vashistha
- **Abstract**: People with disabilities (PwD) experience disproportionately high levels of discrimination and hate online, particularly in India, where entrenched stigma and limited resources intensify these challenges. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to identify and mitigate online hate, yet most research on online ableism focuses on Western audiences with Western AI models. Are these models adequately equipped to recognize ableist harm in non-Western places like India? Do localized, Indic language models perform better? To investigate, we adopted and translated a publicly available ableist speech dataset to Hindi, and prompted eight LLMs--four developed in the U.S. (GPT-4, Gemini, Claude, Llama) and four in India (Krutrim, Nanda, Gajendra, Airavata)--to score and explain ableism. In parallel, we recruited 175 PwD from both the U.S. and India to perform the same task, revealing stark differences between groups. Western LLMs consistently overestimated ableist harm, while Indic LLMs underestimated it. Even more concerning, all LLMs were more tolerant of ableism when it was expressed in Hindi and asserted Western framings of ableist harm. In contrast, Indian PwD interpreted harm through intention, relationality, and resilience--emphasizing a desire to inform and educate perpetrators. This work provides groundwork for global, inclusive standards of ableism, demonstrating the need to center local disability experiences in the design and evaluation of AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the cultural generalizability of AI systems in recognizing ableist harm, focusing on Western and Indic Large Language Models (LLMs). It compares how PwD in the US and India interpret ableist speech, alongside LLM assessments. The study translates an ableist speech dataset into Hindi and prompts eight LLMs (four Western and four Indian) to score and explain ableism. The results reveal significant misalignments: Western LLMs consistently overestimate ableist harm, while Indic LLMs underestimate it. All LLMs demonstrated higher tolerance for ableism expressed in Hindi and applied Western-centric frameworks. In contrast, Indian PwD highlighted the importance of intent, relationality, and resilience, alongside the desire to educate perpetrators. The work underscores the need to center local disability experiences in AI design and evaluation for global inclusivity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions. First, it provides a comparative study of how PwD in India and the US identify and explain ableist speech, highlighting cultural framings related to relationality, intent, and intersectionality. Second, it contributes an ableist speech dataset in Hindi and audits Indian LLMs. Third, the paper reveals how multilingual LLMs lack "multiculturalism" when it comes to disability. These are all significant steps forward, which directly speak to a more global and equitable approach to AI development and cultural bias mitigation.

*   **Significance:** The paper directly tackles a critical bias in human-centered AI, where most research and model development remains focused on Western, Educated, Industrialized, Rich, and Democratic (WEIRD) populations. The study's focus on India and the specific challenges faced by PwD in that region is highly relevant, as this demographic represents a significant portion of the global population. The finding that Western LLMs overestimate harm while Indian LLMs underestimate it exposes concerning biases in model training and raises questions about the reliability of these models for global hate speech detection. By identifying divergences in cultural understandings of ableism and the failures of LLMs to acknowledge nuanced local interpretations (intention, relationality, resilience), the paper raises urgent questions about the design and deployment of AI systems in non-Western contexts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The mixed-methods approach, combining quantitative assessments with qualitative explanations from PwD in different cultural contexts, provides a holistic understanding of the nuances of ableism.
    *   The bilingual analysis of ableist speech in English and Hindi reveals concerning biases in LLMs' understanding of harm across languages.
    *   The study's focus on intersectionality, highlighting how disability intersects with other forms of marginalization (gender, caste, class), adds depth to the analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The sample size of PwD in India is relatively small compared to that in the US, which might limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   While the study includes four Indian LLMs, it is crucial to expand this to different language models and extend to Indic languages beyond Hindi. A study in more languages will allow the construction of a more robust dataset.
    *   The study could benefit from a more thorough exploration of the underlying reasons for the observed biases in LLMs, such as the specific training data used and the cultural assumptions embedded in the models' architecture.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The study has the potential to influence the design and evaluation of AI systems by promoting the inclusion of diverse cultural perspectives and local disability experiences.
    *   It can inform the development of more culturally sensitive and context-aware ableism detection models that are better equipped to recognize and mitigate harm in non-Western contexts.
    *   It could inspire further research on cross-cultural fairness in AI and the development of inclusive AI ethics frameworks.
    *   The data provides an avenue for future research to improve LLM evaluations by training models with datasets from non-Western regions.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed study with novel insights into the cultural generalizability of AI systems in detecting ableist harm. The findings are significant and have the potential to influence the development of more inclusive and equitable AI models. The paper's weaknesses are minor and do not detract significantly from its overall contribution.

- **Score**: 8/10

### SpiroLLM: Finetuning Pretrained LLMs to Understand Spirogram Time Series with Clinical Validation in COPD Reporting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16145v1)
- **Authors**: Shuhao Mei, Yongchao Long, Shan Cao, Xiaobo Han, Shijia Geng, Jinbo Sun, Yuxi Zhou, Shenda Hong
- **Abstract**: Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD), a major chronic respiratory disease with persistent airflow limitation, is a leading global cause of disability and mortality. Respiratory spirogram time series, routinely collected during pulmonary function tests (PFTs), play a critical role in the early detection of repsiratory diseases and in monitoring lung function over time. However, most current AI models for COPD diagnosis are limited to outputting classification results without providing a rationale for their diagnostic process, while current Large Language Models (LLMs) cannot understand spirograms yet, which severely limits their clinical trust and adoption. To tackle this challenge, we leverage a cohort of 234,028 individuals from the UK Biobank (UKB) to propose SpiroLLM, the first multimodal large language model that can understand spirogram. The model extracts morphological features from respiratory curves via a SpiroEncoder and aligns them with PFT numerical values in a unified latent space using a SpiroProjector, ultimately empowering a large language model to generate a comprehensive diagnostic report. Experimental results confirm that SpiroLLM achieved a diagnostic AUROC of 0.8980 (95% CI: 0.8820-0.9132). In a robustness test with missing core data, it maintained a 100% valid response rate, far surpassing the 13.4% of a text-only model and showcasing the superiority of its multimodal design. This work demonstrates the substantial potential of deeply fusing physiological signals with large language models, establishing a new paradigm for the next generation of interpretable and reliable clinical decision support tools.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SpiroLLM: Finetuning Pretrained LLMs to Understand Spirogram Time Series with Clinical Validation in COPD Reporting":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SpiroLLM, a novel multimodal large language model (LLM) designed to understand and generate clinical reports from spirogram time series data for Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) diagnosis.  The model leverages a SpiroEncoder to extract morphological features from respiratory curves, aligns them with pulmonary function test (PFT) numerical values using a SpiroProjector, and uses an LLM to generate comprehensive diagnostic reports. The model is trained on a large dataset from the UK Biobank (UKB) and evaluated using an "LLM-as-a-Judge" approach and expert evaluation. The results show SpiroLLM achieves a high diagnostic AUROC and demonstrates robustness in the presence of missing data, outperforming text-only baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents several innovative aspects:

*   **Multimodal Fusion:** The core novelty lies in its multimodal architecture, specifically integrating visual information from spirogram waveforms with structured PFT data within an LLM framework. This addresses a significant gap in existing AI models for COPD, which typically rely on either classification outputs or textual data.
*   **Automated Report Generation:** The use of LLMs for generating comprehensive diagnostic reports, complete with rationale, is a significant step forward in terms of interpretability and clinical utility. The semi-automated pipeline for creating high-quality training data also contributes to the paper's novelty.
*   **Clinical Validation:** The use of an LLM-as-a-Judge, validated through independent expert review, is a rigorous approach to evaluating the clinical quality of the generated reports, going beyond conventional text-similarity metrics.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Diagnostic Accuracy:** SpiroLLM's strong AUROC score suggests it could enhance the accuracy and efficiency of COPD diagnosis, especially in resource-limited settings. The increased Sensitivity (relative to the pftonly model) is especially valuable.
*   **Enhanced Interpretability:** By generating rationale-backed reports, SpiroLLM promotes clinical trust and adoption, facilitating better communication between AI and healthcare professionals.
*   **Potential for Clinical Decision Support:** SpiroLLM could serve as a powerful decision support tool, assisting clinicians in interpreting complex PFT data and improving patient care.
*   **Robustness and Generalization:** The paper demonstrates SpiroLLM's ability to work even with core data missing. The architecture and techniques can be generalized for applications to other medical time series and potentially extended to other diseases with appropriate task specific fine-tuning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Bias:** The UKB dataset is predominantly of European ancestry. The model's generalization ability to more diverse ethnic populations needs further validation.
*   **Simulated Clinical Environment:** The evaluation relies on retrospective data and automated metrics with LLM judges to assess outputs, and there may be a gap with the judgments of practicing physicians in a real clinical environment, and the results may differ.
*   **Scope Limited to COPD:** The current implementation focuses on a single disease. More tests needs to be explored with other respiratory diseases.
*   **Expertise on Expert LLM Judge Outputs:** Relying on a single Large Language Model for judging might produce outputs that are not the most optimized and factual correct.

**Overall Assessment:**

SpiroLLM is a significant contribution to the intersection of AI and respiratory medicine. It addresses a crucial need for interpretable and robust tools for COPD diagnosis, demonstrating the potential of multimodal LLMs in clinical decision support. While the limitations regarding dataset bias and scope should be considered, the paper showcases a novel architecture and rigorous evaluation methodology with substantial promise for future research and clinical implementation.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### LSSGen: Leveraging Latent Space Scaling in Flow and Diffusion for Efficient Text to Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16154v1)
- **Authors**: Jyun-Ze Tang, Chih-Fan Hsu, Jeng-Lin Li, Ming-Ching Chang, Wei-Chao Chen
- **Abstract**: Flow matching and diffusion models have shown impressive results in text-to-image generation, producing photorealistic images through an iterative denoising process. A common strategy to speed up synthesis is to perform early denoising at lower resolutions. However, traditional methods that downscale and upscale in pixel space often introduce artifacts and distortions. These issues arise when the upscaled images are re-encoded into the latent space, leading to degraded final image quality. To address this, we propose {\bf Latent Space Scaling Generation (LSSGen)}, a framework that performs resolution scaling directly in the latent space using a lightweight latent upsampler. Without altering the Transformer or U-Net architecture, LSSGen improves both efficiency and visual quality while supporting flexible multi-resolution generation. Our comprehensive evaluation covering text-image alignment and perceptual quality shows that LSSGen significantly outperforms conventional scaling approaches. When generating $1024^2$ images at similar speeds, it achieves up to 246\% TOPIQ score improvement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LSSGen: Leveraging Latent Space Scaling in Flow and Diffusion for Efficient Text to Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LSSGen, a novel framework designed to improve the efficiency and quality of text-to-image generation using flow matching and diffusion models. The core idea is to perform resolution scaling directly in the latent space rather than in pixel space. This is achieved using a lightweight latent upsampler, avoiding artifacts often introduced by pixel-space scaling. The framework also incorporates a noise compensation and rescheduling strategy to ensure consistency between noise and data across different scaling stages. The authors demonstrate that LSSGen achieves a significant speedup while maintaining or even improving image quality compared to existing methods, especially at high resolutions. Their experiments cover various generative architectures, including both flow matching and diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of performing resolution scaling in the latent space isn't entirely new, as latent diffusion models (LDMs) exist. However, LSSGen's specific implementation, including the lightweight latent upsampler decoupled from the generative backbone (U-Net or Transformer), noise compensation strategy, and progressive resolution scaling, does present a novel combination of techniques. It's a clever approach to decouple the upsampling process, making it reusable across models. The approach also addresses an existing need in the field – mitigating artifacts introduced by pixel-space upscaling, which is a significant contribution in itself.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in text-to-image generation: the trade-off between image quality and computational cost, particularly at high resolutions. By improving efficiency without sacrificing quality, LSSGen has the potential to make high-resolution image generation more accessible and practical.  The extensive experimental results showcasing LSSGen's performance across different models and resolutions strengthens its significance. The empirical evidence supporting the claimed speedups and quality improvements are solid. Moreover, the analysis comparing latent-space and pixel-space transformations offers valuable insights into multi-resolution image generation. The demonstrated improvement in TOPIQ is particularly significant. The ablation study is also well done, pinpointing the contributions of different components.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing pixel-space scaling methods.
    * **Novel Combination of Techniques:** LSSGen combines latent space scaling, a lightweight upsampler, and noise compensation in an effective way.
    * **Strong Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across different architectures and resolutions support the claims. The use of metrics like GenEval, CLIP-IQA, TOPIQ, and NIQE is appropriate.
    * **Insightful Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the trade-offs and dynamics involved in multi-resolution image generation.
    * **Generalizability:** The decoupling of the upsampler from the generative backbone is a key strength, making it applicable to a wide range of models.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **VAE Dependence:** The method relies on a VAE for latent space representation. While many models use VAEs, it might limit applicability to models that don't. However, this is explicitly mentioned in the paper, so it's not a major flaw, just a limitation.
    * **Over-sharpening:** The authors acknowledge potential over-sharpening at high resolutions, which could be further explored with potential mitigation strategies.
    * **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the authors provide some theoretical justification, a more rigorous theoretical analysis of the noise compensation and rescheduling strategy could further strengthen the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, LSSGen represents a significant contribution to the field of text-to-image generation. The approach is innovative, well-validated empirically, and addresses a key challenge in the field. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. LSSGen is likely to influence future research in efficient and high-quality image generation by providing a practical and generalizable framework for latent space scaling. The detailed ablation study adds further insights. The improvements in TOPIQ score are a critical achievement.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM Data Selection and Utilization via Dynamic Bi-level Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16178v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Yu, Kai Han, Hang Zhou, Yehui Tang, Kaiqi Huang, Yunhe Wang, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: While large-scale training data is fundamental for developing capable large language models (LLMs), strategically selecting high-quality data has emerged as a critical approach to enhance training efficiency and reduce computational costs. Current data selection methodologies predominantly rely on static, training-agnostic criteria, failing to account for the dynamic model training and data interactions. In this paper, we propose a new Data Weighting Model (DWM) to adjust the weight of selected data within each batch to achieve a dynamic data utilization during LLM training. Specially, to better capture the dynamic data preference of the trained model, a bi-level optimization framework is implemented to update the weighting model. Our experiments demonstrate that DWM enhances the performance of models trained with randomly-selected data, and the learned weighting model can be transferred to enhance other data selection methods and models of different sizes. Moreover, we further analyze how a model's data preferences evolve throughout training, providing new insights into the data preference of the model during training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Data Weighting Model (DWM) to dynamically adjust the weights of data samples during the pre-training of Large Language Models (LLMs). The DWM is trained using a bi-level optimization framework, where the inner level optimizes the LLM's parameters based on the weighted data, and the outer level optimizes the DWM to improve the LLM's performance on a validation set. This approach aims to capture the dynamic data preferences of the LLM during training and to consider the joint effects of different data samples within a batch.  Experiments show that incorporating DWM improves the performance of LLMs trained on randomly selected data and can be transferred to other data selection methods and larger models. The authors also analyze the evolution of data preferences throughout training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the dynamic, bi-level optimization approach to data weighting during LLM pre-training. While data selection and weighting strategies are not entirely new, the combination of dynamic weighting based on the *current* model's preferences, updated through bi-level optimization, represents a meaningful advance. Traditional data selection is often static, performed *before* training starts. DWM's ability to adapt during training to a model's changing needs is a significant shift.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve LLM training efficiency and reduce computational costs by focusing on the most relevant data at each stage of training. The transferability of the DWM to different data selection methods and model sizes is a further indication of its practical value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of static data selection methods and motivates the need for a dynamic approach.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The bi-level optimization framework is well-defined and theoretically justified.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments comparing DWM to various baselines on multiple datasets and model sizes.
    *   **Transferability:** Demonstrating the transferability of the DWM is a significant contribution.
    *   **Analysis of Data Preference Evolution:** The analysis of how data preferences change during training provides valuable insights into the learning dynamics of LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Overhead:** While the paper mentions the computational overhead, a more detailed analysis of the training time and resource requirements is necessary to understand the practical impact of DWM. The 9% increase seems reasonable, but more concrete comparisons are warranted.
    *   **Validation Set Sensitivity:** The results may be sensitive to the choice of validation set (as the authors touch upon), which needs further exploration.
    *   **Limited to Pre-training:** The paper focuses solely on pre-training. It would be beneficial to investigate whether DWM could also be applied to fine-tuning.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence research in LLM training by highlighting the importance of dynamic data utilization strategies. Other researchers can build upon this work by exploring different architectures for the DWM, alternative optimization methods, or applications in other NLP tasks. However, the additional computational cost needs further examination to justify real-world adoption.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by addressing a key limitation of current LLM training approaches: the static nature of data selection. The proposed DWM, coupled with the bi-level optimization framework, provides a novel and effective method for dynamically adjusting data weights during pre-training. The experimental results support the effectiveness and transferability of the DWM. While there are weaknesses regarding computational overhead and the dependence on the validation set, the strengths significantly outweigh them.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Emergent Cognitive Convergence via Implementation: A Structured Loop Reflecting Four Theories of Mind (A Position Paper)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16184v1)
- **Authors**: Myung Ho Kim
- **Abstract**: We report the discovery of a structural convergence across four influential theories of mind: Kahneman's dual-system theory, Friston's predictive processing, Minsky's society of mind, and Clark's extended mind-emerging unintentionally within a practical AI agent architecture called Agentic Flow. Designed to address limitations in large language models (LLMs), Agentic Flow comprises five interdependent modules such as Retrieval, Cognition, Control, Memory, and Action arranged in a recurrent cognitive loop. Although originally inspired only by Minsky and Clark, the system's structure retrospectively aligns with computational motifs found in all four theories, including predictive modeling, associative recall, and error-sensitive control. To assess this convergence, we conducted comparative experiments with baseline LLM agents on multi-step reasoning tasks. The structured agent achieved 95.8% task success and exhibited strong constraint adherence, while the baseline system succeeded 62.3% of the time. These results were not aimed at proving superiority, but at illustrating how theoretical structures may emerge through practical design choices rather than top-down theory. We introduce PEACE as a descriptive meta-architecture that captures design-level regularities observed in Agentic Flow. Not intended as a new theory, PEACE provides a shared vocabulary for understanding architectures shaped by real-world implementation demands. This paper should be read as a position paper - an exploratory reflection on how implementation can surface latent structural echoes of cognitive theory, without asserting theoretical unification.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Agentic Flow," a novel AI agent architecture designed to improve the reliability and controllability of Large Language Models (LLMs) by organizing their cognition into five modules: Retrieval, Cognition, Control, Memory, and Action, forming a repeatable cognitive loop. The core claim is that this architecture, developed pragmatically to address LLM limitations, unexpectedly exhibits a structural convergence with four prominent theories of mind: Kahneman's dual-system theory, Friston's predictive processing, Minsky's society of mind, and Clark's extended mind. The paper presents qualitative evidence for this convergence and provides empirical results demonstrating Agentic Flow's superior performance over baseline LLM-based systems in multi-step, conditional reasoning tasks.  A descriptive meta-architecture, PEACE (Predictive, Emergent, Adaptive, Cognitive, Environmental), is introduced to characterize this shared structure. The paper frames this finding not as a theoretical unification, but as an exploratory reflection on how implementation can surface latent architectural commonalities across divergent cognitive theories, suggesting that intelligent architectures may evolve towards shared structural patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interesting Observation:** The core idea of a convergence between a pragmatically-engineered architecture and seemingly disparate high-level cognitive theories is compelling and warrants further investigation.
    *   **Empirical Validation (Partial):** The empirical evaluation provides evidence (although limited in scope) supporting Agentic Flow's improved performance. The comparison against the LLM-only baseline is valuable.
    *   **PEACE Framework:** PEACE, although descriptive, provides a useful framework for comparing architectures across different theoretical backgrounds.  It provides a basis for future, more systematic analyses.
    *   **"Implementation-First" Perspective:** The paper offers a valuable counterpoint to theory-driven AI development, emphasizing the potential for engineering constraints to reveal fundamental principles. This perspective aligns with a growing movement towards embodied and situated AI.
    * **Well-Structured Argument:** The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the motivations, methodology, and conclusions of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Qualitative Convergence Claims:** The "convergence" with the four theories is primarily qualitative and, at times, feels like a *post-hoc* interpretation. While the parallels are intriguing, they lack the rigor of a formal mapping or computational implementation of the theories within Agentic Flow. A more specific analysis indicating *how* a specific mechanism in Agentic Flow *directly* implements an aspect of, for example, Predictive Processing could strengthen the claim. Without this, it borders on high-level metaphor.
    *   **Limited Empirical Scope:** The experimental scenarios are limited in their complexity and the number of tools used. The LLM-only baseline, while useful, might not be the strongest comparator (e.g., comparing against other structured approaches to prompting LLMs). The absence of more diverse and challenging tasks limits the generalizability of the findings.  Furthermore, the success rate is evaluated using human grading of relevance and accuracy, creating a risk of subjective bias.
    *   **Proprietary Implementation:** The reliance on a proprietary implementation raises concerns about reproducibility and independent verification. While the authors offer evaluation protocols, the lack of open-source code significantly hinders scrutiny of the architecture and the experimental setup.  This limits the potential impact of the work.
    *   **Overselling Claims:** The paper occasionally overstates the implications of the findings, particularly concerning Newell's unified theory of cognition. The observed convergence, even if robust, is far from a complete instantiation of such a grand theory.
    *   **Lack of Novelty in some aspects** The overall architecture consists of common components that are being used in existing research and applications. The primary novelty is in the specific implementation and design choice, as well as in the discovery of a structural convergence between the design and cognitive theories.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents an interesting and potentially significant observation about architectural convergence in AI. The empirical results offer preliminary support for the benefits of Agentic Flow's design. However, the qualitative nature of the convergence claims, the limited scope of the empirical evaluation, the reliance on a proprietary implementation, and occasional overstatement of conclusions diminish the overall impact. It's a promising starting point, but requires further rigorous validation and formalization.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** This score reflects the balance between the intriguing nature of the core observation, the promising (though limited) empirical support, and the weaknesses concerning methodological rigor, reproducibility, and the strength of the theoretical claims. The paper is a valuable contribution in terms of raising interesting questions, but requires further, more rigorous, research to substantiate its claims and truly impact the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Do Large Language Models Have a Planning Theory of Mind? Evidence from MindGames: a Multi-Step Persuasion Task
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16196v1)
- **Authors**: Jared Moore, Ned Cooper, Rasmus Overmark, Beba Cibralic, Nick Haber, Cameron R. Jones
- **Abstract**: Recent evidence suggests Large Language Models (LLMs) display Theory of Mind (ToM) abilities. Most ToM experiments place participants in a spectatorial role, wherein they predict and interpret other agents' behavior. However, human ToM also contributes to dynamically planning action and strategically intervening on others' mental states. We present MindGames: a novel `planning theory of mind' (PToM) task which requires agents to infer an interlocutor's beliefs and desires to persuade them to alter their behavior. Unlike previous evaluations, we explicitly evaluate use cases of ToM. We find that humans significantly outperform o1-preview (an LLM) at our PToM task (11% higher; $p=0.006$). We hypothesize this is because humans have an implicit causal model of other agents (e.g., they know, as our task requires, to ask about people's preferences). In contrast, o1-preview outperforms humans in a baseline condition which requires a similar amount of planning but minimal mental state inferences (e.g., o1-preview is better than humans at planning when already given someone's preferences). These results suggest a significant gap between human-like social reasoning and LLM abilities.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces MINDGAMES, a novel "planning theory of mind" (PToM) task designed to evaluate whether agents can infer and strategically intervene on others' mental states to persuade them to alter their behavior. Unlike previous theory-of-mind evaluations that focus on passive prediction, MindGames requires agents to actively plan and persuade. The task involves a persuader (human or LLM) attempting to influence a target (a naively rational bot) to choose between three proposals, with incomplete information and differing preferences. The authors conducted a human experiment and compared performance against several LLMs, including 01-preview. The results indicate that humans significantly outperform LLMs in the PToM task, while 01-preview surpasses humans in a simpler baseline condition, suggesting a gap in human-like social reasoning abilities. The authors further explored task variants to pinpoint the reasons for LLM failure, finding that LLMs struggle to appeal to the target's mental states, a behavior crucial for success in the PToM task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by introducing a novel task, MINDGAMES, that moves beyond passive, predictive assessments of theory of mind to evaluate active, planning-based ToM abilities in LLMs. This addresses a significant limitation of existing ToM benchmarks, which may be susceptible to superficial heuristics and memorization.

The significance lies in highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in social reasoning. The finding that humans significantly outperform LLMs in the more complex PToM task, while LLMs excel in a simpler baseline, suggests that LLMs may lack the implicit causal models of other agents that underpin human social intelligence. While other papers have looked at planning and ToM, the interactive aspect of MindGames is a strong departure. The results presented are convincingly arguing that spectatorial and planning ToM abilities are distinct, which has important theoretical and practical implications.

However, several aspects warrant further consideration.

*   **Naive Rationality of Target**: The target agent is “naively rational”. This simplified behavior, while controlled, limits the ecological validity of the task. It's unclear how the observed differences would translate to more complex, human-like targets. The target also has a simplified expression of its mental state (for example, always stating when asked about informational states).

*   **Scope of Generalization**: The authors acknowledge that their results are specific to their task. Generalizing the findings to broader social reasoning scenarios requires further investigation with diverse task settings. As they acknowledge the limited ecological validity, future experiments might attempt to loosen controls on both the player and target behavior.

*   **Low Human Performance**: While humans outperformed LLMs in the HIDDEN condition, average human performance was still relatively low (29%). This suggests that the task is inherently challenging, even for humans, and raises questions about the practical relevance of the observed differences. It also suggests that LLMs have large headroom for improving performance. It might be necessary to refine the task/instructions to improve human understanding of the game.

*   **Limited LLM Analysis**: The paper focuses primarily on the 01-preview model, with other models discussed but less deeply analyzed. A more detailed comparison of the strengths and weaknesses of different LLM architectures in this task would strengthen the conclusions. It might also be valuable to evaluate the effect of prompting, tuning, and scaffolding and see how the different agents improve.

Despite these points, the paper successfully introduces a new avenue for theory-of-mind research and offers insights about LLMs' limits. This area is likely to be influential for both research and engineering.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the significance of the work, with the caveats previously discussed, I would assign a score of **7/10**. It is a significant contribution, even if limited by the simplicity of the naively-rational target agent. It also does not address possible prompt-engineering work that would further improve or degrade LLM's capabilities. There are also limitations based on the human baseline, for example, if the task's complexity limits its efficacy. Despite this, it offers an insightful perspective on LLMs' active planning capabilities.
