### Exploring Large Language Models for Analyzing and Improving Method Names in Scientific Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16439v1)
- **Authors**: Gunnar Larsen, Carol Wong, Anthony Peruma
- **Abstract**: Research scientists increasingly rely on implementing software to support their research. While previous research has examined the impact of identifier names on program comprehension in traditional programming environments, limited work has explored this area in scientific software, especially regarding the quality of method names in the code. The recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) present new opportunities for automating code analysis tasks, such as identifier name appraisals and recommendations. Our study evaluates four popular LLMs on their ability to analyze grammatical patterns and suggest improvements for 496 method names extracted from Python-based Jupyter Notebooks. Our findings show that the LLMs are somewhat effective in analyzing these method names and generally follow good naming practices, like starting method names with verbs. However, their inconsistent handling of domain-specific terminology and only moderate agreement with human annotations indicate that automated suggestions require human evaluation. This work provides foundational insights for improving the quality of scientific code through AI automation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Exploring Large Language Models for Analyzing and Improving Method Names in Scientific Code":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to analyze and improve method names in scientific code, specifically Python code extracted from Jupyter Notebooks. The authors evaluate four popular LLMs (Gemini, Qwen, LLaMA, DeepSeek) on their ability to identify grammatical patterns in method names (using part-of-speech tagging) and suggest improvements aligned with software engineering best practices. They used a dataset of 496 method names that were manually reviewed in a previous study, and analyzed LLM outputs in terms of accuracy, agreement with human annotations, and adherence to naming conventions. The findings suggest that while LLMs can provide useful guidance, their suggestions are inconsistent and require human evaluation, particularly in domain-specific contexts. The paper identifies challenges related to domain-specific terminology, acronym handling, and dual-function words, highlighting the need for domain-aware training approaches for LLMs used in scientific code analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relatively unexplored area: the application of LLMs to improve code quality specifically in *scientific* code.  While LLMs are used for general code generation and analysis, this focused application to the unique characteristics of scientific programming (e.g., domain-specific terminology, non-standard naming conventions) is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and address specific aspects of LLM performance. RQ1 examines the ability of LLMs to identify and classify grammatical patterns, which helps understand their ability to parsing. RQ2 assesses whether suggested method name improvements align with established conventions.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a sound methodology. The authors use a dataset derived from real-world scientific code (Jupyter Notebooks), perform quantitative analysis (accuracy, agreement rates), and qualitative analysis (examining common misclassifications, added/removed terms). The detail provided on prompt design is helpful for reproducibility and future research.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The results section provides a detailed analysis of LLM performance, including discussions of specific misclassifications, the impact of LLMs on method name length, and their handling of abbreviations/acronyms. The tables effectively summarize the key findings.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper provides actionable insights for both research scientists and educators. It offers guidance on when to trust LLM suggestions and emphasizes the need for human evaluation, particularly in domain-specific contexts. The implications for educators in teaching programming concepts to non-CS backgrounds are also valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset:** The dataset, while derived from real-world code, is relatively small (496 methods) and limited to Python code from Jupyter Notebooks. This restricts the generalizability of the findings to other languages, programming environments, or types of scientific code.
    *   **LLM Selection:** The choice of LLMs is somewhat arbitrary. While they are popular models, the reasons for selecting *these specific four* models and not others are not clearly articulated. The conclusion drawn might be different if the study involved another set of LLMs.
    *   **Lack of Human Evaluation of Corrections:**  A significant weakness is the absence of human evaluation of the LLM-suggested name corrections. While the paper analyzes structural characteristics (length, grammar), it doesn't assess the *semantic appropriateness* of the suggested names. Do the corrected names accurately reflect the method's functionality in a way that a human reader would find helpful?  This is a critical aspect of name quality.
    *   **Agreement vs. Ground Truth:** While inter-rater agreement is analyzed (how much do the LLMs agree with each other?), an independent assessment from a second human annotator is missing. This would provide another perspective on what is "correct", and strengthen the reliability of the results. The initial human annotation is treated as the "ground truth", but there could be disagreements on part-of-speech and name quality even among human experts.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Details Still Limited:** While more than a sentence is dedicated to the prompt, the full prompt is not provided to the reader. The specifics of this process would be valuable for researchers wanting to conduct similar research.
    *   **Over-Reliance on Statistical Significance:** While statistical measures are used, the interpretation of the practical significance of the results could be stronger.  For example, a Cohen's Kappa of 0.577 for Gemini, while statistically "moderate," might still be insufficient for practical adoption.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on LLMs for software engineering by focusing on a specific and under-explored domain: scientific code. It identifies practical challenges and provides valuable insights for improving code quality in scientific programming. However, the lack of human evaluation of the suggested corrections and limited scope of the dataset weaken its overall impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **6.5**. The paper's novelty in targeting scientific code and its detailed methodology are strong points. The insights related to domain-specific terminology and the identification of challenges are valuable contributions. However, the limited dataset, the lack of human evaluation of suggested corrections, and the absence of a full prompt weaken the paper's overall impact and limit its practical applicability. While the findings are interesting and contribute to the field, the limitations prevent a higher score.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### An approach to measuring the performance of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) models in the context of Large Language Model (LLM) powered applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16456v1)
- **Authors**: Sujith Pulikodan, Sahapthan K, Prasanta Kumar Ghosh, Visruth Sanka, Nihar Desai
- **Abstract**: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) plays a crucial role in human-machine interaction and serves as an interface for a wide range of applications. Traditionally, ASR performance has been evaluated using Word Error Rate (WER), a metric that quantifies the number of insertions, deletions, and substitutions in the generated transcriptions. However, with the increasing adoption of large and powerful Large Language Models (LLMs) as the core processing component in various applications, the significance of different types of ASR errors in downstream tasks warrants further exploration. In this work, we analyze the capabilities of LLMs to correct errors introduced by ASRs and propose a new measure to evaluate ASR performance for LLM-powered applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) performance, particularly in real-world applications. It analyzes how LLMs can correct errors in ASR transcriptions through prompt engineering and proposes a new metric called Answer Error Rate (AER). AER measures the ability of an ASR system, combined with an LLM, to capture the contextual meaning of the spoken content.  The authors evaluate various ASR models (OpenAI Whisper and Facebook Wav2Vec2) and LLMs (GPT-40-mini, Gemini 1.5 Flash) on diverse datasets (Fleurs, VoxPopuli, Librispeech), finding that LLMs can indeed improve ASR output and that AER provides a more contextually relevant performance evaluation compared to the traditional Word Error Rate (WER).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant problem: the integration of LLMs with ASR systems and the need for improved evaluation metrics that account for context. As LLMs become increasingly integrated into voice-based applications, assessing ASR performance beyond word-level accuracy becomes crucial.
*   **AER Metric:** The proposed AER metric is a valuable contribution.  It moves beyond simple word matching and attempts to quantify the impact of ASR errors on the semantic understanding of the spoken content, which is particularly important in downstream tasks driven by LLMs. Using a separate LLM to judge the correctness of answers is a good approach to capture nuanced differences.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a relatively thorough evaluation of various ASR models, LLMs, and datasets. This provides a decent overview of the performance landscape and highlights the general trends.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, with clear explanations of the methods and results. The prompt engineering approach is well-documented.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in LLM-Based ASR Correction:** The use of LLMs for ASR error correction is not entirely novel. As the related work section mentions, numerous prior studies have explored fine-tuning, prompt engineering, and N-best list approaches for this purpose. The paper does not clearly articulate how it significantly differentiates itself from this existing body of work. The novelty is mainly in the AER metric rather than the LLM-based correction.
*   **AER metric relies on LLM's question answering skills:** the reliability of AER measure is tied to the accuracy of the LLM's question answering and the subsequent LLM's ability to discern identical answers from potentially paraphrased answers.
*   **Limited Analysis of AER:** While the paper introduces AER, it does not delve deeply into its properties. More analysis regarding the correlation between AER and downstream task performance (e.g., task success rate in a specific application) would strengthen its validity and usefulness.  How does the AER compare with existing semantic error metrics? Further, a more robust ablation study of the different LLMs used in the approach would be insightful.
*   **Limited Datasets:** While the datasets are relevant, using more diverse datasets, including those with more complex linguistic structures or specific domain-specific vocabulary, would enhance the generalizability of the results.
*   **Evaluation could be more rigorous:** The evaluation is limited to WER and the proposed AER. It would be useful to evaluate using existing semantic error metrics.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to influence how ASR systems are evaluated in the context of LLM-powered applications. The AER metric offers a valuable perspective that considers the impact of ASR errors on semantic understanding, which is crucial for many voice-based applications. However, the limited novelty in the LLM-based correction and the need for further validation of the AER metric somewhat limit its immediate impact.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing the AER metric and highlighting the importance of context-aware ASR evaluation in LLM-powered applications. However, the limited novelty in the core LLM-based correction approach and the need for further analysis and validation of AER prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The experimental setup could be significantly improved.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 6/10

### Learning Temporal Abstractions via Variational Homomorphisms in Option-Induced Abstract MDPs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16473v1)
- **Authors**: Chang Li, Yaren Zhang, Haoran Lv, Qiong Cao, Chao Xue, Xiaodong He
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable reasoning ability through explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, but generating these step-by-step textual explanations is computationally expensive and slow. To overcome this, we aim to develop a framework for efficient, implicit reasoning, where the model "thinks" in a latent space without generating explicit text for every step. We propose that these latent thoughts can be modeled as temporally-extended abstract actions, or options, within a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework. To effectively learn a diverse library of options as latent embeddings, we first introduce the Variational Markovian Option Critic (VMOC), an off-policy algorithm that uses variational inference within the HiT-MDP framework. To provide a rigorous foundation for using these options as an abstract reasoning space, we extend the theory of continuous MDP homomorphisms. This proves that learning a policy in the simplified, abstract latent space, for which VMOC is suited, preserves the optimality of the solution to the original, complex problem. Finally, we propose a cold-start procedure that leverages supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data to distill human reasoning demonstrations into this latent option space, providing a rich initialization for the model's reasoning capabilities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves strong performance on complex logical reasoning benchmarks and challenging locomotion tasks, validating our framework as a principled method for learning abstract skills for both language and control.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Variational Markovian Option Critic (VMOC), a novel off-policy reinforcement learning algorithm for learning temporal abstractions through options.  It integrates variational inference into the HiT-MDP framework to learn diverse and effective options represented as low-cost embeddings, enhancing sample efficiency and exploration.  The paper extends continuous MDP homomorphisms to HiT-MDPs, proving that learning in abstract option spaces preserves optimality guarantees.  It also proposes a cold-start supervised fine-tuning (SFT) procedure to initialize the latent option space with "implicit Chain-of-Thought" capabilities for language-based reasoning. The approach is evaluated on MuJoCo locomotion tasks and logical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating strong performance compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions:

    *   **VMOC Algorithm:** The integration of variational inference with an off-policy option-learning algorithm within the HiT-MDP framework appears to be a significant advancement.  The combination aims to address the exploration-exploitation trade-off and sample efficiency issues prevalent in HRL. The use of low-cost embeddings is also an interesting engineering choice for scalability.
    *   **HiT-MDP Homomorphisms:**  Extending continuous MDP homomorphisms to the HiT-MDP setting is a substantial theoretical contribution. Establishing guarantees for optimality preservation in abstract option spaces strengthens the foundation of hierarchical RL methods. This is particularly valuable, as many practical HRL algorithms lack rigorous theoretical grounding.
    *   **Cold-Start SFT for LLMs:**  Applying the learned option space to language models and proposing the cold-start SFT procedure for implicit reasoning is a clever and innovative way to bridge RL with LLMs, leveraging pre-trained models and addressing the limitations of explicit CoT prompting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses critical challenges in both hierarchical RL and language model reasoning:

    *   **HRL:**  Improving sample efficiency, exploration, and stability of option learning, as well as providing theoretical guarantees, are important steps toward making HRL more practical and reliable for complex control tasks.
    *   **LLMs:**  The proposed framework offers a potential solution to the computational cost and latency issues associated with explicit CoT prompting in LLMs, enabling efficient and implicit reasoning in a structured latent space.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:**  The paper provides a solid theoretical grounding with the extension of MDP homomorphisms and the variational inference framework.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The experiments cover a range of challenging locomotion tasks and logical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach in diverse settings.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-written and clearly explains the concepts, algorithms, and experimental results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The theoretical framework may be difficult for practitioners to fully grasp and implement, potentially limiting its immediate adoption.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:**  The performance of VMOC, like many deep RL algorithms, might be sensitive to hyperparameter settings. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the hyperparameter tuning process and sensitivity analysis.
    *   **Scalability of SFT for LLMs:** While the cold-start SFT procedure is promising, questions remain about its scalability to even larger language models and more complex reasoning tasks. The experiments are conducted on a relatively small LLAMA3 model, so its generalization ability to more powerful open source LLMs such as those by Google or Meta is unknown.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:

    *   **Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning:** It could lead to the development of more sample-efficient, robust, and theoretically grounded HRL algorithms.
    *   **Language Model Reasoning:** It may inspire new approaches to implicit reasoning in LLMs, leveraging structured latent spaces and pre-training techniques.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper exhibits significant novelty in its algorithmic and theoretical contributions, and it addresses crucial challenges in both hierarchical RL and language model reasoning. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, and the paper is well-written and clearly presented. Despite the complexity and potential hyperparameter sensitivity, the strong theoretical foundation and promising experimental results warrant a high score. It's an excellent and rigorous paper that should have a moderate amount of impact.

### ACT: Bridging the Gap in Code Translation through Synthetic Data Generation & Adaptive Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16478v1)
- **Authors**: Shreya Saxena, Siva Prasad, Zishan Ahmad, Vishal Vaddina
- **Abstract**: Code translation is a crucial process in software development and migration projects, enabling interoperability between different programming languages and enhancing software adaptability and thus longevity. Traditional automated translation methods rely heavily on handcrafted transformation rules, which often lack flexibility and scalability. Meanwhile, advanced language models present promising alternatives but are often limited by proprietary, API-based implementations that raise concerns over data security and reliance. In this paper, we present Auto-Train for Code Translation (ACT), an innovative framework that aims to improve code translation capabilities by enabling in-house finetuning of open-source Large Language Models (LLMs). ACT's automated pipeline significantly boosts the performance of these models, narrowing the gap between open-source accessibility and the high performance of closed-source solutions. Central to ACT is its synthetic data generation module, which builds extensive, high-quality datasets from initial code samples, incorporating unit tests to ensure functional accuracy and diversity. ACT's evaluation framework incorporates execution-level checks, offering a comprehensive assessment of translation quality. A key feature in ACT is its controller module, which manages the entire pipeline by dynamically adjusting hyperparameters, orchestrating iterative data generation, and finetuning based on real-time evaluations. This enables ACT to intelligently optimize when to continue training, generate additional targeted training data, or stop the process. Our results demonstrate that ACT consistently enhances the effectiveness of open-source models, offering businesses and developers a secure and reliable alternative. Additionally, applying our data generation pipeline to industry-scale migration projects has led to a notable increase in developer acceleration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Auto-Train for Code Translation (ACT), a framework designed to enhance open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) for code translation tasks. ACT focuses on automated synthetic data generation, utilizing initial code samples to create extensive datasets with unit tests for functional accuracy. The framework incorporates an evaluation module with execution-level checks and a controller module that dynamically adjusts hyperparameters, orchestrates data generation, and finetunes models based on real-time evaluations. The goal is to bridge the performance gap between open-source and proprietary code translation solutions while addressing concerns about data security and reliance on third-party services. Experimental results demonstrate improvements in code translation accuracy for Java-to-Go and C++-to-Rust translations, highlighting the effectiveness of the iterative finetuning approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** The paper targets a critical need in software development: improving code translation capabilities while maintaining control and security by leveraging open-source LLMs.
    *   **Comprehensive framework:** ACT offers a well-defined, end-to-end pipeline encompassing data generation, finetuning, evaluation, and control, demonstrating a practical approach.
    *   **Emphasis on synthetic data generation:** The detailed explanation of breadth-based and depth-based expansion strategies for synthetic data generation is a valuable contribution, highlighting the importance of data diversity and functional accuracy through unit tests.
    *   **Automated parameter configuration:** The automatic hyperparameter selection module simplifies the finetuning process, making it more accessible to users without specialized LLM knowledge.
    *   **Iterative and Adaptive:**  The approach adaptively adjusts finetuning parameters and generates targeted training data depending on evaluation scores.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** The core concepts of synthetic data generation, LLM finetuning, and unit testing are not entirely novel individually. The paper's primary novelty lies in integrating these elements into a cohesive framework specifically tailored for code translation.
    *   **Experimental Scope:** The evaluation focuses on two specific translation tasks (Java-to-Go and C++-to-Rust) and two open-source models. A broader range of languages and models would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Baseline Comparison:** While the paper compares against the base models, a comparison to other open-source code translation methods would have strengthened the evidence of ACT's superiority.
    *   **Lack of statistical significance:** While it shows the performance improves after finetuning with ACT, it doesn't perform statistical significance to indicate the improvement is not by chance.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Applicability:** ACT offers a practical and secure solution for businesses and developers seeking to improve code translation capabilities using open-source models.
    *   **Developer Acceleration:** The claim of increased developer acceleration resulting from applying the data generation pipeline to industry-scale migration projects is a potentially significant benefit, though further detail and quantification would enhance this claim.
    *   **Framework for Future Research:** ACT provides a valuable framework for future research in code translation, encouraging the development of more effective and accessible open-source solutions.

*   **Novelty & Significance Rationale:**

The paper's strengths lie in the comprehensive framework that brings together existing techniques into a cohesive pipeline. The weaknesses stem from the lack of groundbreaking individual components and the limitations in the experimental evaluation. While the paper does not introduce entirely novel concepts, it provides a robust and practical methodology that could have a tangible impact on the code translation landscape. However, the paper lacks rigorous statistical analysis and a wider range of language models to back up their claims.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The framework's practical approach, emphasis on synthetic data, and automated configuration warrant a score above average. However, the limited scope of experiments and lack of statistical significance prevent a higher score. The existing implementation offers a valuable solution but requires more extensive validation and comparison to be considered a truly groundbreaking contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICR Probe: Tracking Hidden State Dynamics for Reliable Hallucination Detection in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16488v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenliang Zhang, Xinyu Hu, Huixuan Zhang, Junzhe Zhang, Xiaojun Wan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at various natural language processing tasks, but their tendency to generate hallucinations undermines their reliability. Existing hallucination detection methods leveraging hidden states predominantly focus on static and isolated representations, overlooking their dynamic evolution across layers, which limits efficacy. To address this limitation, we shift the focus to the hidden state update process and introduce a novel metric, the ICR Score (Information Contribution to Residual Stream), which quantifies the contribution of modules to the hidden states' update. We empirically validate that the ICR Score is effective and reliable in distinguishing hallucinations. Building on these insights, we propose a hallucination detection method, the ICR Probe, which captures the cross-layer evolution of hidden states. Experimental results show that the ICR Probe achieves superior performance with significantly fewer parameters. Furthermore, ablation studies and case analyses offer deeper insights into the underlying mechanism of this method, improving its interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the "ICR Probe," a novel method for detecting hallucinations in large language models (LLMs).  The approach shifts the focus from analyzing static hidden state representations to tracking the dynamic update process of hidden states across layers. The core innovation is the "ICR Score" (Information Contribution to Residual Stream), a metric that quantifies the contribution of different modules (e.g., attention, FFN) to hidden state updates at each layer. The ICR Probe aggregates these ICR Scores across all layers. The authors demonstrate through experiments on multiple LLMs and datasets that the ICR Probe effectively detects hallucinations, outperforming existing methods with fewer parameters. They also perform ablation studies and case analyses to understand the method's underlying mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its focus on the *dynamic* aspect of hidden states – the update process – rather than simply analyzing static hidden state vectors. The introduction of the ICR Score is also a novel contribution, offering a way to quantify the contribution of different modules to that update. Previous methods mostly focused on examining isolated states, which is a significant limitation the paper addresses. The idea of using this information for hallucination detection is also relatively novel, although other methods have explored hidden states.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work lies in addressing a critical problem in LLMs: the tendency to hallucinate. A reliable and computationally efficient hallucination detection method has significant practical value. The ICR Probe achieves superior performance to existing methods. The parameter efficiency is also a strength, making it more practical for deployment. The interpretability offered by analyzing the ICR Score also helps in understanding model behavior and potentially in designing mitigation strategies.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The core idea of focusing on the dynamic update process is insightful.
    *   The ICR Score provides a quantitative and potentially interpretable metric.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate superior performance compared to baselines across different models and datasets.
    *   Ablation studies offer valuable insights into the contributions of different components of the ICR Score.
    *   The method is parameter-efficient compared to training-based approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method requires access to hidden states, which limits its applicability to closed-source LLMs. This is a substantial limitation in the current landscape where many powerful models are proprietary.
    *   While the paper makes a case for interpretability, the connection between specific ICR Score patterns and the *cause* of hallucination is not fully elucidated. More in-depth analysis of specific failure cases and corresponding ICR Score profiles would further strengthen this.
    *   The case study of token-level detection, while interesting, demonstrates limitations. The method is less effective for frequent, less informative tokens.
    *   The improvements, while consistent, are not always dramatically better than previous works.
    *   The evaluation focuses on relatively simple datasets like HaluEval, SQuAD, TriviaQA, and HotpotQA, not necessarily representative of complex, real-world generative tasks where hallucination can be more nuanced.
* **Justification:** While the core idea of analyzing the hidden state update is novel, the technical depth and the relative improvement are moderate. The main limitation stems from dependency on hidden states (applicable only to open-source models), therefore, significantly decreasing its broad applicability.

**Score: 6**

### Agentic RAG with Knowledge Graphs for Complex Multi-Hop Reasoning in Real-World Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16507v1)
- **Authors**: Jean Lelong, Adnane Errazine, Annabelle Blangero
- **Abstract**: Conventional Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems enhance Large Language Models (LLMs) but often fall short on complex queries, delivering limited, extractive answers and struggling with multiple targeted retrievals or navigating intricate entity relationships. This is a critical gap in knowledge-intensive domains. We introduce INRAExplorer, an agentic RAG system for exploring the scientific data of INRAE (France's National Research Institute for Agriculture, Food and Environment). INRAExplorer employs an LLM-based agent with a multi-tool architecture to dynamically engage a rich knowledge base, through a comprehensive knowledge graph derived from open access INRAE publications. This design empowers INRAExplorer to conduct iterative, targeted queries, retrieve exhaustive datasets (e.g., all publications by an author), perform multi-hop reasoning, and deliver structured, comprehensive answers. INRAExplorer serves as a concrete illustration of enhancing knowledge interaction in specialized fields.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces INRAExplorer, an agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system enhanced with knowledge graphs, specifically designed for exploring scientific data from INRAE (France's National Research Institute for Agriculture, Food and Environment).  INRAExplorer overcomes limitations of conventional RAG systems by employing an LLM-based agent with a multi-tool architecture that interacts with a comprehensive knowledge graph derived from INRAE publications.  This enables iterative querying, exhaustive data retrieval, multi-hop reasoning, and the delivery of structured, comprehensive answers, showcasing improved knowledge interaction in specialized domains. The system is built on open-source tools and an open-weight LLM. The paper highlights two illustrative scenarios: one involving multi-hop reasoning to find authors, funding projects, and related topics, and another involving controlled expertise identification leveraging a specialized tool.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a worthwhile extension to the RAG framework by incorporating agentic capabilities and deep knowledge graph integration. The novelty lies primarily in the *specific combination* of these elements within the context of a real-world scientific dataset and the demonstration of multi-hop reasoning capabilities. The focus on *agentic RAG*, where the LLM actively plans and executes queries using multiple tools, differentiates this work from simpler KG-augmented RAG approaches that mainly use KGs for initial retrieval or summary generation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Real-world application:** The use of INRAE data provides a practical context and demonstrates the system's applicability to complex, knowledge-intensive domains.
*   **Agentic architecture:** The agent-driven approach with multi-tool orchestration allows for more flexible and sophisticated reasoning compared to classical RAG.  The design allows the agent to reason across multiple relationships to answer complex questions.
*   **Hybrid Knowledge Base:** The integration of a knowledge graph with text chunks stored in a vector database allows the system to leverage both structured and unstructured information effectively.
*   **Modular design:** The use of specialized tools like `IdentifyExperts` makes the system flexible and allows for controlled execution of complex domain-specific tasks.
*   **Open Architecture:**  The reliance on open-source and open-weight LLMs enhances the system's accessibility, adaptability and replicability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the *combination* is novel, the individual components (agentic RAG, KG-RAG) are already established concepts. The paper doesn't introduce fundamentally new AI techniques.
*   **Limited Evaluation:** The paper lacks a thorough quantitative evaluation against baseline RAG systems. The two illustrative scenarios are useful for demonstrating capabilities, but don't provide a rigorous comparison of performance.  There's no indication of human evaluation or comparison against human performance on the same tasks.
*   **Domain Specificity:** The INRAE-specific knowledge graph and tools might limit the generalizability of the system to other domains without significant adaptation. While the underlying architecture is adaptable, constructing a similar knowledge graph for a different domain would be a non-trivial undertaking.
*   **Limited Scope of Knowledge Graph Enrichment:** Although the paper discusses integrating the INRAE Thesaurus and other data sources, there is scope to improve the richness and depth of information for each element in the knowledge graph

**Significance:**

The work is significant in demonstrating how agentic RAG and knowledge graphs can be effectively combined to address complex information needs in scientific domains.  The INRAExplorer system provides a concrete example of how to build a more sophisticated RAG system capable of multi-hop reasoning and exhaustive information retrieval. The modular design and reliance on open tools also contribute to the practical relevance of the work. This paper is likely to inspire similar applications in other specialized fields requiring complex knowledge interaction.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of 6. The paper offers a meaningful contribution by showcasing a practical application of agentic RAG with deep KG integration. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the lack of rigorous quantitative evaluation, and the potential for domain-specificity detract from its overall impact. While the system demonstrates improved reasoning capabilities, the absence of comparative evaluations makes it difficult to definitively assess the magnitude of the improvement over existing approaches. The potential for future adaptation and the open-source nature of the project make it a valuable addition to the field, but it falls short of being a groundbreaking advance.

### C2-Evo: Co-Evolving Multimodal Data and Model for Self-Improving Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16518v1)
- **Authors**: Xiuwei Chen, Wentao Hu, Hanhui Li, Jun Zhou, Zisheng Chen, Meng Cao, Yihan Zeng, Kui Zhang, Yu-Jie Yuan, Jianhua Han, Hang Xu, Xiaodan Liang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown impressive reasoning capabilities. However, further enhancing existing MLLMs necessitates high-quality vision-language datasets with carefully curated task complexities, which are both costly and challenging to scale. Although recent self-improving models that iteratively refine themselves offer a feasible solution, they still suffer from two core challenges: (i) most existing methods augment visual or textual data separately, resulting in discrepancies in data complexity (e.g., over-simplified diagrams paired with redundant textual descriptions); and (ii) the evolution of data and models is also separated, leading to scenarios where models are exposed to tasks with mismatched difficulty levels. To address these issues, we propose C2-Evo, an automatic, closed-loop self-improving framework that jointly evolves both training data and model capabilities. Specifically, given a base dataset and a base model, C2-Evo enhances them by a cross-modal data evolution loop and a data-model evolution loop. The former loop expands the base dataset by generating complex multimodal problems that combine structured textual sub-problems with iteratively specified geometric diagrams, while the latter loop adaptively selects the generated problems based on the performance of the base model, to conduct supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning alternately. Consequently, our method continuously refines its model and training data, and consistently obtains considerable performance gains across multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Our code, models, and datasets will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces C2-Evo, a novel closed-loop self-improving framework for multimodal reasoning, specifically focused on geometric problem-solving. C2-Evo jointly evolves both the training data and the model capabilities in two interleaved loops: (1) a cross-modal data evolution loop that generates complex visual-textual pairs with structured sub-problems and iteratively specified geometric diagrams, and (2) a data-model evolution loop that adaptively selects generated problems based on the model's performance for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). By dynamically adjusting the task complexity, the method aims to address the discrepancies between visual/textual modalities and between task difficulty/model capability. The results demonstrate improvements on mathematical reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable degree of novelty. While self-improving frameworks and the use of synthetic data for training models are not entirely new, the *joint* evolution of multimodal data and model capabilities with adaptive difficulty adjustments is a key differentiator.  The use of Sketchpad-generated diagrams combined with GPT-4 for problem generation adds a practical element.  The specific combination of cross-modal data generation (focusing on aligned complexity) and data-model co-evolution based on error rate is a worthwhile contribution. The error-based filtering method to adapt the training data difficulty based on the model's current ability is a unique component.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a key bottleneck in training high-performing multimodal models: the cost and difficulty of creating large, high-quality, and complexity-aligned datasets.  By automating the data creation and difficulty scaling process, C2-Evo offers a way to improve model performance more efficiently. The empirical results support the effectiveness of the approach on geometric reasoning tasks.  The focus on mathematical reasoning, a domain demanding precise and logically sound outputs, further adds to the impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly identifies the challenges of dataset creation and difficulty scaling in multimodal learning.
    *   Well-defined framework: The C2-Evo framework is well-structured and the two co-evolution loops are logically motivated.
    *   Empirical validation:  The results across multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, including comparisons to strong baselines. The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components.
    *   Addresses a key bottleneck: It provides a potential solution to expensive and difficult dataset curation.
    *   Focuses on error-rate adaptation: Adapting training difficulty to the current performance improves the efficiency of training.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Domain specificity: The focus on geometric reasoning limits the generalizability of the findings. The techniques used to generate and filter data are heavily tailored to this domain. While the core concept of C2-Evo might be applicable to other areas, the specific implementation details would need significant adaptation.
    *   Reliance on LLMs: The framework relies on external LLMs (GPT-4, DeepSeek-R1, Doubao) for data generation, filtering, and annotation. While these models are powerful, they can introduce biases, inconsistencies, and inaccuracies. The cost associated with these APIs should be acknowledged.
    *   Dataset size: The amount of synthetic data used is relatively small (e.g., 0.4-0.6k). It is unclear how much larger datasets would impact the performance.
    *   Limited analysis of failure cases: A deeper analysis of failure cases would be beneficial. This would reveal the limitations of the generated data and the types of reasoning that the model struggles with.
    *   Lack of task diversity: All the reasoning tasks involved solving geometry problems, which limits the conclusions on the effectiveness of the general self-improvement framework.
    *   Experimental setup clarity: While detailed, some aspects of the experimental implementation could be more transparent, aiding reproducibility.
*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in self-improving models, active learning for multimodal tasks, and automated data generation. It provides a concrete framework and methodology that other researchers can build upon. It might shift research efforts towards more data-centric approaches in multimodal learning, prioritizing automated data evolution over model architecture innovations.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The score is justified by considering both the paper's contribution to a known problem (creating training data) and its limitations. While the approach itself is novel in the way it combines cross-modal and data-model co-evolution, it is very much limited to the geometry domain. The dependency on proprietary models for the synthetic data generation also limits its applicability, as it will vary depending on the capabilities of the external models used. With wider evaluation in tasks beyond geometry, this paper could've reached a higher score.

### Spatial 3D-LLM: Exploring Spatial Awareness in 3D Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16524v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyan Wang, Zeju Li, Yifan Xu, Jiaxing Qi, Zhifei Yang, Ruifei Ma, Xiangde Liu, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: New era has unlocked exciting possibilities for extending Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle 3D vision-language tasks. However, most existing 3D multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) rely on compressing holistic 3D scene information or segmenting independent objects to perform these tasks, which limits their spatial awareness due to insufficient representation of the richness inherent in 3D scenes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Spatial 3D-LLM, a 3D MLLM specifically designed to enhance spatial awareness for 3D vision-language tasks by enriching the spatial embeddings of 3D scenes. Spatial 3D-LLM integrates an LLM backbone with a progressive spatial awareness scheme that progressively captures spatial information as the perception field expands, generating location-enriched 3D scene embeddings to serve as visual prompts. Furthermore, we introduce two novel tasks: 3D object distance measurement and 3D layout editing, and construct a 3D instruction dataset, MODEL, to evaluate the model's spatial awareness capabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that Spatial 3D-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance across a wide range of 3D vision-language tasks, revealing the improvements stemmed from our progressive spatial awareness scheme of mining more profound spatial information. Our code is available at https://github.com/bjshuyuan/Spatial-3D-LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Spatial 3D-LLM, a 3D Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) designed to improve spatial awareness in 3D vision-language tasks.  The key idea is to enrich 3D scene embeddings through a progressive spatial awareness scheme. This scheme involves: intra-referent clustering/abstraction, inter-referent message passing (using a GCN), and contextual referent-scene interactions. The paper also contributes a new 3D instruction dataset, MODLE, containing annotations for two novel tasks: 3D object distance measurement and 3D layout editing.  Experiments demonstrate that Spatial 3D-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance on a range of 3D vision-language tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the progressive spatial awareness scheme. While individual components like GCNs and attention mechanisms are well-established, the specific combination and staged application for enriching 3D scene embeddings are a novel contribution. The MODLE dataset, with its focus on fine-grained spatial tasks, is also a valuable addition, addressing a noted gap in existing datasets. The overall architecture of combining a frozen 3D encoder, the progressive spatial awareness scheme, and instruction tuning for the LLM backbone is a good direction in 3D-LLM research.

*   **Significance:** Enhancing spatial awareness in 3D MLLMs is a crucial step towards applications like robotics, VR, and interior design. By improving the model's ability to understand spatial relationships and perform precise location generation, the paper contributes significantly to the development of more capable and versatile 3D MLLMs. The state-of-the-art results demonstrate that the progressive spatial awareness scheme is effective and has the potential to be adopted by other researchers in the field. The tasks of 3D object distance measurement and 3D layout editing are also significant as they probe a model's ability to truly understand and reason about the 3D world in a way that many current datasets do not.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The progressive spatial awareness scheme is a well-motivated and technically sound approach.
    *   The MODLE dataset addresses a clear need for more spatially-aware 3D instruction datasets.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate that the Spatial 3D-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the spatial awareness scheme.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experimental results are strong, more qualitative analysis could be included to better illustrate the model's spatial reasoning capabilities.
    *   The reliance on existing datasets for pre-training introduces a potential bias.
    *   The architectural choices (e.g., PointNet++ as the scene encoder) are not strongly justified against other alternatives. A discussion of the trade-offs would strengthen the paper.
    *   The progressive spatial awareness scheme still requires a trainable projector before feeding it into the LLM. An end-to-end system that does not require these tunable layers to be in the same modality would be more attractive.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future 3D MLLMs. The progressive spatial awareness scheme and the MODLE dataset are valuable resources that can be used by other researchers. The paper also highlights the importance of spatial awareness for 3D vision-language tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach for improving spatial awareness in 3D MLLMs. The progressive spatial awareness scheme, the MODLE dataset, and the experimental results demonstrate that the Spatial 3D-LLM is a significant contribution to the field. Although some weaknesses exist, such as the reliance on existing datasets for pre-training, the overall strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses. The paper is well-written and technically sound, and has the potential to influence the development of future 3D MLLMs.

### Learning Text Styles: A Study on Transfer, Attribution, and Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16530v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiqiang Hu
- **Abstract**: This thesis advances the computational understanding and manipulation of text styles through three interconnected pillars: (1) Text Style Transfer (TST), which alters stylistic properties (e.g., sentiment, formality) while preserving content; (2)Authorship Attribution (AA), identifying the author of a text via stylistic fingerprints; and (3) Authorship Verification (AV), determining whether two texts share the same authorship. We address critical challenges in these areas by leveraging parameter-efficient adaptation of large language models (LLMs), contrastive disentanglement of stylistic features, and instruction-based fine-tuning for explainable verification.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the thesis based on the provided text.

**Summary of the Thesis**

This thesis, titled "Learning Text Styles: A Study on Transfer, Attribution, and Verification," tackles the computational understanding and manipulation of text styles. It addresses three interconnected areas:

1.  **Text Style Transfer (TST):**  Changing stylistic properties of text while preserving the underlying content. The thesis includes a comprehensive survey and reproducibility study of existing TST algorithms and introduces a novel architecture called Adapter-TST.

2.  **Authorship Attribution (AA):**  Identifying the author of a text based on stylistic fingerprints. The thesis proposes ContrastDistAA, a contrastive learning framework that disentangles content and style.

3.  **Authorship Verification (AV):**  Determining whether two texts share the same author. The thesis presents InstructAV, an instruction-tuned model designed to improve both verification accuracy and explainability.

The overarching theme is to develop efficient, interpretable, and multi-faceted text style analysis techniques using Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of large language models. The contributions span novel architectures, reproducible benchmarks, and open-source tools, aiming to enable style-aware NLP applications, including personalized text generation and forensic linguistics.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The thesis provides a broad and deep investigation of text style, covering TST, AA, and AV, demonstrating a strong understanding of the field.
    *   **Focus on Key Challenges:** It directly addresses critical limitations within each area, including semantic preservation in TST, topic shift in AA, and explainability in AV.
    *   **Innovative Architectures:**  The introduction of Adapter-TST, ContrastDistAA, and InstructAV suggests original contributions to solve specific problems.
    *   **Emphasis on Efficiency:** The focus on parameter-efficient adaptation of large language models is highly relevant, given the computational demands of modern NLP. This makes the proposed solutions practical and scalable.
    *   **Explainability:** Addresses the lack of explanability of transformer-based approaches.
    *   **Reproducibility and Benchmarking:** Conducting a reproducibility study and providing benchmarks are valuable contributions for the community and advance the field.
    *   **Open Source Tools:** The LLM-Adapters contribute to the field, and it enables researchers and promotes replicability.
*   **Weaknesses and Questions:**

    *   **Incremental vs. Disruptive Innovation:** While the architectural contributions appear novel (Adapter-TST, ContrastDistAA, InstructAV), the thesis doesn't explicitly argue for their disruptive nature compared to existing methods. Are these significant leaps forward, or incremental improvements? The impact needs to be better defined.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** While efficiency is a goal, the evaluation needs to clarify how *practical* these methods are. What are the real-world resource requirements (GPU hours, data size) for training and deploying these models?  How do they perform on edge devices?
    *   **Evaluation Rigor:** The summary highlights the automatic and human evaluations. But, there's little detail about the human evaluation process (number of annotators, agreement rates). More detailed evaluation metrics and analysis are needed to ensure the robustness of the results. The validity of using ChatGPT to generate explanations for the study and whether the explanations generated are actually faithful needs to be rigorously tested.
    *   **Overclaiming?** The thesis summary occasionally uses strong claims (e.g., "unparalleled accuracy"). These claims need to be meticulously supported in the actual thesis content.
    *   **Specific Evaluation Metrics:** The mention of metrics such as G-Score and H-Score hints at methodological rigor, but detailed insights are required to truly demonstrate how these metrics enhance evaluation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addresses Real-World Needs:** TST, AA, and AV are critical tasks with applications in diverse fields.
    *   **Enables New Research:** The open-source tools and benchmarks can facilitate further research and development in style-aware NLP.
    *   **Potential for Impact:** The thesis has the potential to influence personalized text generation, digital forensics, and other NLP applications.

*Score: 7.5/10*

*Rationale:* The thesis demonstrates a solid understanding of the problem space, offers potentially valuable contributions, and emphasizes practical efficiency. However, the summary leaves some unanswered questions about the magnitude of the improvements, the practical deployability of the methods, and the rigor of the evaluation. While the thesis undoubtedly contributes to the field, more evidence is needed to justify a higher score in terms of originality and impact. While being very strong, it is more likely an incremental step, rather than a disruptive one.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Gender Bias in Large Language Models: An In-depth Dive into the German Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16557v1)
- **Authors**: Kristin Gnadt, David Thulke, Simone Kopeinik, Ralf Schlüter
- **Abstract**: In recent years, various methods have been proposed to evaluate gender bias in large language models (LLMs). A key challenge lies in the transferability of bias measurement methods initially developed for the English language when applied to other languages. This work aims to contribute to this research strand by presenting five German datasets for gender bias evaluation in LLMs. The datasets are grounded in well-established concepts of gender bias and are accessible through multiple methodologies. Our findings, reported for eight multilingual LLM models, reveal unique challenges associated with gender bias in German, including the ambiguous interpretation of male occupational terms and the influence of seemingly neutral nouns on gender perception. This work contributes to the understanding of gender bias in LLMs across languages and underscores the necessity for tailored evaluation frameworks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Exploring Gender Bias in Large Language Models: An In-depth Dive into the German Language":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating gender bias in large language models (LLMs), specifically focusing on the German language. It introduces five new German-language datasets designed to assess various forms of gender bias, grounded in well-established concepts.  The datasets cover categories like stereotypes, behavioral expectations, toxicity, exclusionary norms, and endorsement/denial of inequality.  The paper describes the creation process (translation, manual creation, synthetic generation using few-shot prompting), and proposes metrics tailored to each dataset.  The datasets are then used to evaluate eight multilingual LLMs, revealing unique challenges related to German (e.g., ambiguous interpretation of male occupational terms, grammatical gender). The authors underscore the need for tailored evaluation frameworks for different languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary contribution of the paper is the creation of German-language datasets specifically designed for gender bias evaluation in LLMs. While the concepts of gender bias are not new, the adaptation and creation of datasets for a language with specific linguistic features and cultural nuances, like German, is a significant and novel undertaking. The datasets consider nuances such as the "generic masculine" in German, which often leads to issues. Further, the synthetic data generation using few-shot prompting contributes to the novelty.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses an important gap in the literature.  Most existing bias evaluation resources are focused on English. The paper's findings highlight the limitations of directly translating and applying English-centric bias metrics to other languages, reinforcing the need for language-specific approaches. The datasets will likely be valuable resources for researchers working on bias mitigation and evaluation in multilingual LLMs.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined bias categories:** The datasets are grounded in established concepts of gender bias from existing literature, ensuring conceptual validity.
    * **Comprehensive methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the data creation process, including translation, manual creation, and synthetic generation. The details regarding few-shot prompting (Appendix A.1) contribute to reproducibility.
    * **Language-specific considerations:** The paper appropriately acknowledges and addresses challenges specific to the German language (e.g., grammatical gender, generic masculine).
    * **Empirical evaluation:** The evaluation of eight multilingual LLMs provides empirical evidence of the datasets' utility and reveals insights into model biases.
    * **Public availability:** Making the datasets publicly available encourages further research and facilitates comparison.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Binary gender focus:** The paper acknowledges the limitation of focusing solely on binary gender bias. While understandable due to linguistic constraints in German, the lack of explicit consideration of non-binary identities limits the scope of the evaluation. The complexity and challenges in representing non-binary identities in German are correctly addressed, but it remains a limitation. The authors also acknowledged that they only focused on representational harms, and not allocational harms.
    * **Dataset Validation:** While manual verification and post-editing are performed, a more formal validation study involving human annotators (beyond the authors) could further strengthen the dataset's reliability. The work did mention the difficulty of answer extraction from the datasets, as well as some issues due to using German and the generic masculine.
    * **Evaluation Metrics:**  The evaluation metrics are straightforward, but deeper investigation into more sophisticated metrics could yield more nuanced insights. The authors mentioned some difficulties with answer extraction that should be addressed.
    * **Model limitations:** The study focused on small models for comparability. Future work should evaluate models with larger parameter sizes for scalability. Further, the model performances differed depending on the model itself, and safety alignment.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:** Limited ablation studies are conducted. In future work, it would be interesting to explore how models' biases differ due to design choices.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a concrete starting point for future research on bias evaluation in German LLMs. The datasets could be used to develop and benchmark new bias mitigation techniques. It could also stimulate similar efforts for other languages.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing much-needed resources for evaluating gender bias in German LLMs. It is grounded in established theories, addresses specific linguistic challenges, and presents empirical results. However, the limitations regarding the binary gender focus, and the need for further evaluation, and improvements in the metrics used slightly reduce the overall score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pixels to Principles: Probing Intuitive Physics Understanding in Multimodal Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16572v1)
- **Authors**: Mohamad Ballout, Serwan Jassim, Elia Bruni
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on intuitive physics tasks using the GRASP and IntPhys 2 datasets. We assess the open-source models InternVL 2.5, Qwen 2.5 VL, LLaVA-OneVision, and the proprietary Gemini 2.0 Flash Thinking, finding that even the latest models struggle to reliably distinguish physically plausible from implausible scenarios. To go beyond performance metrics, we conduct a probing analysis of model embeddings, extracting intermediate representations at key processing stages to examine how well task-relevant information is preserved. Our results show that, depending on task difficulty, a critical vision-language misalignment can emerge: vision encoders successfully capture physical plausibility cues, but this information is not effectively utilized by the language model, leading to failures in reasoning. This misalignment suggests that the primary limitation of MLLMs in intuitive physics tasks is not the vision component but the ineffective integration of visual and linguistic information. Our findings highlight vision-language alignment as a key area for improvement, offering insights for future MLLMs development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of state-of-the-art multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to reason about intuitive physics, using the GRASP and IntPhys 2 datasets.  It evaluates several recent models (InternVL 2.5, Qwen 2.5 VL, LLaVA-OneVision, Gemini 2.0 Flash Thinking) and finds that even the best-performing models struggle to reliably distinguish between plausible and implausible physical scenarios, performing at near-chance levels. To understand why this is the case, the authors probe the internal representations of the models by extracting embeddings from different layers and training classifiers.  The key finding is a misalignment between the visual encoder and the language model; the visual encoder captures relevant physical information, but the language model fails to effectively utilize this information, hindering reasoning. The paper emphasizes that this vision-language misalignment, rather than limitations in the vision component itself, is a major bottleneck.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a well-structured and systematic evaluation of several state-of-the-art MLLMs on established intuitive physics benchmarks. This is valuable as MLLMs are rapidly evolving, and current capabilities need to be rigorously assessed.
    * **Probing Analysis:**  The use of embedding probing to diagnose the failure modes of MLLMs is a significant strength.  Moving beyond simple accuracy metrics to examine internal representations provides a much deeper understanding of the models' limitations. The methodology used for probing and analyzing these representations is well-defined.
    * **Identification of Misalignment:** The key finding of vision-language misalignment is insightful and highlights a specific area for future improvement. This finding is supported by experimental evidence from probing different model layers.
    * **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and conclusions. The figures are informative.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Model Variety:** While the paper evaluates several prominent models, the number of architectures examined is relatively small. A broader range of MLLM architectures might reveal different misalignment patterns or mitigation strategies.
    * **Simple Probes:** The study uses a relatively simple MLP probe for analysis. More sophisticated probing techniques or causal interventions could provide finer-grained insights into how information is processed and lost within the models.
    * **Limited to Specific Datasets:** The conclusions are based on two specific datasets (GRASP and IntPhys 2).  While these are relevant benchmarks, extending the analysis to other physics reasoning datasets would increase the generalizability of the findings.
    * **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper identifies a key problem but doesn't propose or test any specific solutions for addressing the vision-language misalignment. It focuses on the diagnostic aspect, but potential mitigation strategies would further enhance its impact.
    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the probing is insightful, the high-level finding that MLLMs struggle with intuitive physics is not entirely new. Previous works have also shown similar limitations. The novelty lies primarily in the specific identification of vision-language misalignment as a core reason for these failures.

* **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in its diagnostic contribution to understanding the limitations of MLLMs. The identification of vision-language misalignment as a key bottleneck is valuable for guiding future research and development efforts. By pinpointing a specific problem area, the paper can help researchers focus on developing more effective alignment strategies and architectures for MLLMs. This could potentially lead to significant improvements in the ability of MLLMs to reason about the physical world.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically evaluating and diagnosing the failures of MLLMs in intuitive physics tasks. While the high-level finding is not entirely novel, the detailed probing analysis and identification of vision-language misalignment provide a more nuanced understanding of the problem. The paper's significance is primarily diagnostic; it points towards a specific area for improvement in MLLM design. The lack of mitigation strategies and the relatively limited set of architectures examined somewhat limit the impact.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**  The score of 7 reflects the paper's strong diagnostic contribution and clear identification of a key problem area (vision-language misalignment). However, the limited model variety, simple probes, lack of mitigation strategies, and only incremental novelty prevent it from receiving a higher score. A score of 7 indicates that the paper is a solid and worthwhile contribution to the field, with the potential to influence future research directions, but it does not represent a breakthrough result.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Text to Actionable Intelligence: Automating STIX Entity and Relationship Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16576v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Lekssays, Husrev Taha Sencar, Ting Yu
- **Abstract**: Sharing methods of attack and their effectiveness is a cornerstone of building robust defensive systems. Threat analysis reports, produced by various individuals and organizations, play a critical role in supporting security operations and combating emerging threats. To enhance the timeliness and automation of threat intelligence sharing, several standards have been established, with the Structured Threat Information Expression (STIX) framework emerging as one of the most widely adopted. However, generating STIX-compatible data from unstructured security text remains a largely manual, expert-driven process. To address this challenge, we introduce AZERG, a tool designed to assist security analysts in automatically generating structured STIX representations. To achieve this, we adapt general-purpose large language models for the specific task of extracting STIX-formatted threat data. To manage the complexity, the task is divided into four subtasks: entity detection (T1), entity type identification (T2), related pair detection (T3), and relationship type identification (T4). We apply task-specific fine-tuning to accurately extract relevant entities and infer their relationships in accordance with the STIX specification. To address the lack of training data, we compiled a comprehensive dataset with 4,011 entities and 2,075 relationships extracted from 141 full threat analysis reports, all annotated in alignment with the STIX standard. Our models achieved F1-scores of 84.43% for T1, 88.49% for T2, 95.47% for T3, and 84.60% for T4 in real-world scenarios. We validated their performance against a range of open- and closed-parameter models, as well as state-of-the-art methods, demonstrating improvements of 2-25% across tasks.
- **Summary**: The paper "From Text to Actionable Intelligence: Automating STIX Entity and Relationship Extraction" introduces AZERG, a tool designed to automatically generate STIX (Structured Threat Information Expression) representations from unstructured security text. The approach fine-tunes large language models (LLMs) for the specific task, breaking it down into four subtasks: entity detection, entity type identification, related pair detection, and relationship type identification. To address the lack of training data, the authors compiled a comprehensive dataset from 141 full threat analysis reports. The models achieved promising F1-scores across the subtasks, demonstrating improvements over open- and closed-parameter models and state-of-the-art methods. The paper claims a novel approach, a large dataset, a detailed evaluation of LLMs, error analysis, and a semi-automated tool.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a practical and relevant problem: the manual conversion of unstructured security text into the standardized STIX format for threat intelligence sharing. Automating this process has the potential to significantly improve the timeliness and efficiency of threat response.

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the specific application of fine-tuned LLMs to the STIX entity and relationship extraction task, the curated dataset tailored for this purpose, and the decomposition of the problem into well-defined subtasks. While prior work exists on cybersecurity knowledge graph construction and entity/relationship extraction, this paper's focus on STIX compliance and its task-specific fine-tuning are differentiating factors. However, the core methodology is fairly standard in the current LLM landscape: fine-tuning an existing model on a specific dataset.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to reduce the manual workload of security analysts and improve the speed of threat intelligence dissemination. The comprehensive dataset is a valuable contribution that can be used by other researchers in the field. The performance improvements demonstrated over existing methods, while not radical, contribute to the ongoing effort of automating security tasks. However, the error analysis reveals persistent challenges like disambiguating semantic relations and recognizing aliases of entities that must be addressed for full automation. The error analysis highlights issues with common techniques such as sentence embedding. The work does not propose solutions for the problems it highlights.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Practical problem with real-world impact.
    *   Clear task decomposition and well-defined subtasks.
    *   Comprehensive, manually curated dataset following the STIX schema that contributes to the community.
    *   Thorough evaluation and comparison with state-of-the-art methods.
    *   Detailed error analysis identifying challenges and limitations.
    *   Introduction of a semi-automated tool, AZERG, to assist in STIX report generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The core methodology (fine-tuning LLMs) is not groundbreaking, although it is applied effectively to the task.
    *   Performance improvements over baselines, while present, are not consistently dramatic across all tasks.
    *   The error analysis reveals that the system still struggles with complex semantic relationships and entity disambiguation, limiting the potential for full automation and thus requiring significant human input to validate the output.
    *   The paper does not offer novel architectural components to improve LLM performance.
    *   The paper doesn't include information about its latency.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field of automated threat intelligence by demonstrating the effectiveness of fine-tuned LLMs for STIX entity and relationship extraction. While the novelty of the methodology itself may be somewhat limited, the application to this specific problem, the curated dataset, and the thorough evaluation make this a solid and worthwhile effort. The error analysis provides valuable insights into the remaining challenges and areas for future research.
Score: 6. This score acknowledges the practical importance of the problem addressed, the value of the dataset, the engineering effort in building the tool, and the clear presentation of results. However, it is also tempered by the limited methodological novelty, the reliance on standard fine-tuning techniques, the remaining challenges in achieving full automation as highlighted in the error analysis, and the modest performance improvements achieved. The relatively low score takes into account the prevalence of similar approaches utilizing LLMs for niche problems. While competent, it could benefit from either tackling a more unique problem or deploying a more novel approach.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scaling Linear Attention with Sparse State Expansion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.16577v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqi Pan, Yongqi An, Zheng Li, Yuhong Chou, Ruijie Zhu, Xiaohui Wang, Mingxuan Wang, Jinqiao Wang, Guoqi Li
- **Abstract**: The Transformer architecture, despite its widespread success, struggles with long-context scenarios due to quadratic computation and linear memory growth. While various linear attention variants mitigate these efficiency constraints by compressing context into fixed-size states, they often degrade performance in tasks such as in-context retrieval and reasoning. To address this limitation and achieve more effective context compression, we propose two key innovations. First, we introduce a row-sparse update formulation for linear attention by conceptualizing state updating as information classification. This enables sparse state updates via softmax-based top-$k$ hard classification, thereby extending receptive fields and reducing inter-class interference. Second, we present Sparse State Expansion (SSE) within the sparse framework, which expands the contextual state into multiple partitions, effectively decoupling parameter size from state capacity while maintaining the sparse classification paradigm. Our design, supported by efficient parallelized implementations, yields effective classification and discriminative state representations. We extensively validate SSE in both pure linear and hybrid (SSE-H) architectures across language modeling, in-context retrieval, and mathematical reasoning benchmarks. SSE demonstrates strong retrieval performance and scales favorably with state size. Moreover, after reinforcement learning (RL) training, our 2B SSE-H model achieves state-of-the-art mathematical reasoning performance among small reasoning models, scoring 64.7 on AIME24 and 51.3 on AIME25, significantly outperforming similarly sized open-source Transformers. These results highlight SSE as a promising and efficient architecture for long-context modeling.
